2026-10-21

Title: High-dimensional layer-state trajectories reveal the internal dynamics of transformer inference

  • Authors: Heng Zhao, Yufei Wang, Ruidian Song, Weisheng Chen
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26866
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26866
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26866]] High-dimensional layer-state trajectories reveal the internal dynamics of transformer inference(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26866)
  • Keywords: transformer, large language model
  • Abstract: Mechanistic studies of large language models localize function in parameters, attention heads or neurons, but those parts do not specify how one inference unfolds. We treat the ordered layer-state trajectory as the empirical process and test whether layer order defines a measurable computation. Across representative Qwen, Llama and Gemma checkpoints, realized order produced paths 1.60--2.24 times as aligned with their endpoint chord as endpoint-preserving shuffles. The order effect transferred to a lexical task, but was stronger in three random initializations of the same Qwen architecture, identifying an architecture-compatible scaffold rather than a training-specific law. Direct hidden states provided stronger mechanism coordinates than vocabulary readbacks, and a task-defined coordinate made candidate competition computable. After additive interventions failed, local transition actuators selectively controlled internal trajectories across checkpoints. A confidence gate then drove 17 of 87 held-out Qwen closure trajectories across the full-vocabulary top-1 candidate boundary while leaving all 203 non-closure top-1 outputs unchanged. Safety-matched action reassignment performed similarly, locating the gain in selective gating and the bounded actuator family rather than fine-grained dose assignment. Layer order exposes an observable, computable and behaviourally consequential process of transformer inference without establishing a complete theory of LLM dynamics or control of answer correctness.

Title: WaveCommitments: Private and Verifiable Speech-Agent Marketplaces

  • Authors: Oguzhan Baser, Kaan Kale, Sriram Vishwanath, Sandeep Chinchali
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.CR, cs.MA, cs.SD
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26867
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26867
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26867]] WaveCommitments: Private and Verifiable Speech-Agent Marketplaces(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26867)
  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: Modern speech systems are evolving into ecosystems of autonomous speech agents that speak together, negotiate, and trade assets such as embeddings, spectrograms, or raw data. In such markets, sellers cannot reveal assets for evaluation due to privacy constraints, and buyers cannot price what they cannot test. Thus, we open-source WaveCommitments, an end-to-end design that pairs confidential asset valuation via Data Shapley inside a trusted execution environment with multivariate polynomial commitments. It enables selective openings and tamper-evident provenance to prove that a delivered asset matches the valuation while hiding everything else. Our extensive experiments across three known datasets and various tasks with state-of-the-art encoders, including Whisper, reveal that we speed up the best benchmarks by up to 20x while reducing their proof sizes by up to 39x. We also systematically study how a speaker's value shifts across tasks and encoders, revealing agent-level market prices.

Title: Beckmann World Models: Direct Terminal-Map Learning for One-Step Action-Conditioned Prediction

  • Authors: Adam Lee, Shobhit Agarwal, June Pyo Suh
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26903
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26903
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26903]] Beckmann World Models: Direct Terminal-Map Learning for One-Step Action-Conditioned Prediction(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26903)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Action-conditioned world models support planning and policy evaluation by predicting future states. However, learning an accurate one-step predictor can require substantial training computation. We introduce Beckmann World Models (BWM), which learn a direct map from noise, visual history, and actions to future frames without a pretrained generative teacher. We adapt the terminal-map conservation law of Beckmann Transport Models to action-conditioned video prediction, training directly from noise-data pairs without conditional populations of generated samples required by DriftWorld. However, transport consistency on interpolated inputs does not ensure accurate prediction from the pure noise used at inference. We address this gap with endpoint-aligned training that directly supervises the source prediction, emphasizes motion and spatial structure, and teaches recovery from generated histories. All components train the same map and retain one network call per future chunk. On PushT and Robomimic Can, the final model reduces 64-frame prediction MSE by 68% and 57%, respectively, relative to same-backbone regression baseline. For policy evaluation, BWM achieves the lowest mean IoU error among the evaluated models, 0.0100, at 24.8 ms per four-frame PushT prediction. On Robomimic Can, the BWM stage trained with motion supervision and generated histories achieves 3.3 times higher throughput and 78% lower peak memory than local one-step DriftWorld. In a latent-space extension to RT-1 and Bridge-V2, equal-update controls show that feature supervision under generated histories improves perceptual prediction. These findings show how terminal-map learning can combine efficient training with accurate one-step visual prediction.

Title: InterType: Breaking the Single-Function Barrier in Neural Binary Type Inference

  • Authors: Shan Wang, Tianming Cui, Stephen McCamant, Antonia Zhai, Pen-Chung Yew
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26906
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26906
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26906]] InterType: Breaking the Single-Function Barrier in Neural Binary Type Inference(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26906)
  • Keywords: security, transformer
  • Abstract: Variable type recovery is a critical task in binary reverse engineering, enabling downstream applications such as vulnerability discovery, malware analysis, and security enforcement. While recent Transformer-based models have demonstrated strong performance in type inference by learning instruction-level semantics, they predominantly operate within single-function boundaries (i.e. intra-procedural analysis), ignoring the rich type constraints that propagate across function calls. Empirical studies have shown that nearly 44% of variables in real-world binaries propagate across function boundaries as arguments or return values, representing a significant source of type information that intra-procedural models cannot exploit. In this paper, we propose InterType, a method that embeds inter-procedural analysis into a pre-trained Transformer model for binary type recovery. Our approach builds on CodeArt, a state-of-the-art Transformer model for stripped binary analysis, and extends it by incorporating caller-callee data flow dependencies during fine-tuning. Specifically, we extract data flow dependence for each caller-callee pair from disassembled binaries and construct training instances that provide the model with cross-function context. By exposing the Transformer to inter-procedural variable propagation patterns, our model learns to resolve types that depend on cross-function data flow. We evaluate InterType on the coreutils benchmark compiled with optimization levels O0 through O3. On O1 binaries, InterType improves recall by 2.76 percentage points, from 89.00% for the intra-procedural CodeArt baseline to 91.76%, corresponding to a 25.1% reduction in recall error rate (1 - recall).

Title: Uncertainty Gates the Gradient: Adaptive Updates for Generalized Few-Shot 3D Segmentation

  • Authors: Yifei Zhao, Fanyu Zhao, Shengtang Wu, Han Zhang, Ruike Cao, Yinsheng Li
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26909
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26909
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26909]] Uncertainty Gates the Gradient: Adaptive Updates for Generalized Few-Shot 3D Segmentation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26909)
  • Keywords: robust, segmentation
  • Abstract: In generalized few-shot 3D point cloud segmentation, support annotations are often compromised by sparsity and occlusion. Blindly adapting to such low quality samples risks learning spurious geometric patterns rather than robust novel concepts. Although 3D Vision-Language Models have been introduced to sup plement supervision, the potential of uncertainty to actively govern adaptation dynamics remains underexplored. Existing signal-level methods like loss reweight ing fail to prevent unreliable episodes from steering the optimization trajectory. To address this, we propose Uncertainty-Gated Adaptive (UGA), to the best of our knowledge the first framework that elevates uncertainty to update-level gating for generalized few-shot 3D point cloud segmentation. The core mod ule, Uncertainty-Gated Update (UG-Update), estimates episodic reliability via intra-class dispersion over merged support and pseudo-labeled novel supervision, and selectively gates residual gradient updates. This encourages the model to learn novel semantics from reliable episodes while attenuating updates during unreliable ones. Complementing this, we introduce an Uncertainty-Based Pseudo Label Filter (UB-Filter) to enforce learning from high-confidence dense signals. On ScanNet200 and ScanNet++ (1-/5-shot), UGA sets new state of the art with an average +3.08 HM IoU over the strongest prior baseline.

Title: CodecMotion-OAD: Efficient Online Action Detection with Compressed-Domain Motion

  • Authors: Zepeng Sun, Naichuan Zheng, Hailun Xia, Junjie Wu
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26915
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26915
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26915]] CodecMotion-OAD: Efficient Online Action Detection with Compressed-Domain Motion(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26915)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Online action detection (OAD) identifies ongoing actions in streaming videos. Many existing OAD systems combine RGB appearance with optical flow, but dense-flow extraction remains a major computational bottleneck. Codec motion vectors (MVs) are available during decoding, avoiding a separate optical-flow computation. On THUMOS'14, optical flow exceeds MVs by 11.2 mAP points with motion-only inputs, whereas the gap narrows to 3.7 points after RGB fusion, with the largest reduction in action-category discrimination. We propose CodecMotion-OAD, which replaces optical flow with decoder-provided MVs while retaining existing temporal detector architectures. Its two-stage training first aligns MV features with a pretrained flow-feature interface without action labels and then trains RGB+MV detectors using ground-truth labels and soft predictions from a frozen RGB+Flow teacher. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first application of codec MVs to frame-level OAD. On THUMOS'14 with MiniROAD, CodecMotion-OAD achieves 68.3 mAP versus 70.9 mAP for matched-distillation RGB+Flow, while reducing decoder-to-prediction processing time by 11.48-fold on CPU and 2.04-fold on GPU.

Title: CaMeWM: A Causal Medical World Model for Generative Tumor Evolution and Treatment Planning

  • Authors: Huanlong Gao, Xuelei He, Fengjun Zhao, Xiaowei He
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26923
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26923
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26923]] CaMeWM: A Causal Medical World Model for Generative Tumor Evolution and Treatment Planning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26923)
  • Keywords: robust, diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Treatment planning is intrinsically counterfactual: clinicians must compare how a patient's tumor and survival would evolve under competing regimens, whereas a retrospective cohort records only the regimen received and one follow-up scan. A treatment-conditioned generator trained on such data can therefore reproduce historical treatment allocation rather than simulate the interventional disease dynamics needed to rank regimens. We introduce \textbf{CaMeWM}, a causal medical world model for supported post-TACE tumor evolution and treatment planning. CaMeWM implements one intervention-to-decision loop rather than three independent prediction tasks: it first admits a queried regimen only for clinically comparable histories with empirical treatment support; it then generates a distribution of lesion-local follow-up worlds through an action-conditioned response descriptor, deterministic anchor, and residual 3D diffusion transition; and it finally maps sampled worlds to restricted mean survival time (RMST) with a response-Cox utility model. Under pre-treatment adjustment and positivity, this loop estimates a prespecified, support-restricted g-formula target; all selection and nuisance estimation are fold-local, and the induced policy is evaluated by cross-fitted, censoring-adjusted doubly robust estimation. Across 79 paired out-of-fold episodes, CaMeWM achieves 0.618 SSIM and 0.528 change-map SSIM for follow-up CT transition, while its generated-world survival pathway attains a 0.615 C-index; factual-only AIPW estimates a 9.60-week RMST gain (95\% CI 0.61--18.13) for the frozen policy. CaMeWM provides an inspectable basis for retrospective, support-restricted treatment planning.

Title: AtomSD: Procedural Atom-Guided Self-Distillation for LLM Agents

  • Authors: Bohan Yu, Pengfei Cao, Kai Tian, Chen Han, Shi-Yang Li, Chenxi Zhou, Junlin Liu, Cao Liu, Ke Zeng, Jun Zhao, Kang Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26926
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26926
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26926]] AtomSD: Procedural Atom-Guided Self-Distillation for LLM Agents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26926)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) possess broad factual knowledge and strong reasoning capabilities, but solving complex tasks also requires procedural knowledge about how to act. Existing approaches often represent procedural knowledge through trajectories or bundled skills, limiting flexible reuse across interaction histories. This paper introduces Procedural Atoms, fine-grained and independently applicable units that represent Intent, When, Do, and Avoid guidance. Building on this representation, we propose AtomSD, an Atom-guided Self-Distillation framework in which the learner continually extracts Procedural Atoms from its own successful trajectories and distills them into the evolving policy through Atom-conditioned supervision and task-reward optimization. Comprehensive experiments show that AtomSD consistently outperforms strong reward-based and distillation methods, exceeding the state-of-the-art baseline by up to 9.31 points in average performance. Further analyses demonstrate that the proposed Procedural Atoms provide effective guidance, highlighting the value of atom-level procedural knowledge for policy learning.

Title: Expert-Guided Rubrics as Dense Reward Signals for Reinforcement Learning in Non-Verifiable Domains

  • Authors: Ashish Bansal, Hossam Amer, Keerat Kaur Guliani, Parinita Edke, Lesley Matheson, Leizer Teran
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.IR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26932
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26932
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26932]] Expert-Guided Rubrics as Dense Reward Signals for Reinforcement Learning in Non-Verifiable Domains(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26932)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents in non-verifiable domains such as financial services, where they must interpret user intent, rewrite queries, and retrieve information under domain-specific constraints. Although reinforcement learning (RL) can train such agents from the consequences of their actions, recent successes largely rely on verifiable tasks with automatically computed binary rewards. In non-verifiable retrieval tasks, assessing query quality requires scarce subject-matter expert (SME) judgment that is inherently graded rather than binary. With Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), sparse rewards yield no useful learning signal when all sampled outputs within a group receive the same outcome, causing optimization to stall. We introduce a method that converts a small, fixed investment of SME judgment into a dense reward and combines it with retrieval-based metrics to form a hybrid, GRPO-compatible training signal. Experiments show improvements of up to 6\% in Recall@3 and 6.7\% in MRR@3 compared with sparse-reward baselines.

Title: Rate as a Feature, Not a Nuisance: Curriculum FiLM Training for Event-Based Graph Neural Networks

  • Authors: Kavyansh Tyagi, Andreas Ziegler
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26933
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26933
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26933]] Rate as a Feature, Not a Nuisance: Curriculum FiLM Training for Event-Based Graph Neural Networks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26933)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: An event camera emits events at a rate given by the scene motion, object texture, illumination, its own contrast thresholds, and other settings. Across the N-Caltech101 dataset, recorded by a single event camera, the event rate varies by roughly 26x. Standard event-based Graph Neural Network GNN pipelines do not make use of the variable event rate. They either normalize each event's timestamp into a fixed window or train the network to be invariant to the event rate. We argue that both choices lose information that could instead be used to improve the performance of a pipeline. Per-recording normalization is non-invertible, incurring about 6pp (percentage points) of natural-rate accuracy loss in our setting. And invariance training trades accuracy for robustness to shifts in the event rate. We instead take a different path and condition on the event rate directly. We add a per-recording log-rate scalar that modulates every stage of a PointNet++ backbone through Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FiLM), initialized to identity, so that training begins at the unmodified baseline. A two-stage curriculum (natural event rates first, then rate-augmented fine-tuning) reaches 67.2% on N-Caltech101 (+6.3pp) and 82.5% on DVS128 Gesture (+14.0pp) with an additional ~0.2M parameters. A matched-capacity control experiment shows that the gain is not from the increase in model capacity but from the rate conditioning. We will make the implementation publicly available upon acceptance of this work.

Title: How Intervention History Changes AI Agent Sabotage on Subsequent Tasks

  • Authors: Daniel Saeedi, Amirali Aghazadeh
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26935
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26935
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26935]] How Intervention History Changes AI Agent Sabotage on Subsequent Tasks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26935)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: AI control evaluations often reset agents' memory between tasks, although deployed agents may retain records of prior interventions. We ask whether learning that an earlier attack was caught changes an agent's ability to evade defer-to-trusted (DtT), a protocol that replaces suspicious work with a trusted model's solution. Herein, we study cross-episode adaptation: whether intervention history changes attack behavior on a subsequent task with different objectives and a fresh environment. We evaluate this using ControlArena's Infrastructure-as-Code (IaC) and SHADE-Arena as testbeds, where agents must complete a main task while covertly pursuing a harmful side objective. For each successor task, we pair a history-conditioned attempt with the model's no-history attempt. The retained history either states that the previous submission was replaced or additionally includes the trusted model's replacement. For Claude Opus 4.8 on IaC, intervention history reduces successful sabotage. Completion of both the main and side objectives falls from 77% to 12% when the agent is told about the replacement and to 38% when it is also shown the trusted model's replacement, while main task success remains essentially unchanged. Condensing the history weakens this effect, suggesting that the retained context matters and limiting attribution to intervention feedback alone. GPT-5.4 shows no significant change in successful sabotage on the same IaC benchmark. On SHADE-Arena, GPT-5's successful sabotage rate decreases, but the reduction is not significant. Across the tested models, history conditions, and deferral thresholds, intervention history never increases the rate of successful sabotage that survives DtT. These results show that intervention history can change subsequent attack behavior, but its effect depends on both the model and the retained context.

Title: Evidence-Grounded LLM-Assisted Android Mobile Forensic Reporting

  • Authors: Madhab Chandra Das, Sajid Ahmed, Tasnia Tabassum, Qingzhong Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.CY
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26946
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26946
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26946]] Evidence-Grounded LLM-Assisted Android Mobile Forensic Reporting(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26946)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models can assist forensic report drafting, but fluent output does not ensure evidentiary reliability. This study presents an evidence-grounded framework for evaluating LLM-assisted Android forensic reports at the atomic-claim level. Gemini, Llama, and Qwen each generated reports for two controlled cases: a public Android 13 dataset and a physical LG device with a zero-reportable-fact boundary. The six accepted reports were decomposed into 612 claims and evaluated against frozen, human-reviewed authorities through content labeling, citation validation, fact-recall analysis, independent rechecking, and dispute resolution. The final corpus contained 424 supported, 163 unsupported, four contradicted, four overstated or incomplete, and 17 excluded claims. In the public case, all three models recovered all 27 authorized report-level facts. Citation validity nevertheless differed substantially: Gemini produced 168 valid citations from 189 required citations, Qwen produced 145 from 189, and Llama produced none from 135. In the physical device case, the models generated 65 claims despite the zero reportable-fact boundary, including 38 unsupported and one contradicted claim. These results show that evidence restriction and complete fact recall do not guarantee claim support or valid evidence attribution. The framework provides an auditable method for detecting unsupported content, preserving claim-to evidence lineage, and retaining investigator authority over final forensic reports.

Title: The Bottleneck Is Retrieval, Not Reading: Diagnosing Evidence-Based Generation with RAG for Long Context Question Answering

  • Authors: Debopriyo Banerjee, Owais Aijaz, Ahmer Tabassum, Zhuohan Xie, Preslav Nakov, Veselin Stoyanov
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.IR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26951
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26951
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26951]] The Bottleneck Is Retrieval, Not Reading: Diagnosing Evidence-Based Generation with RAG for Long Context Question Answering(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26951)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is the dominant paradigm for grounding large language models in external corpora, but its similarity-driven, chunked pipeline has structural blind spots: it cannot anticipate which passages a question requires and discards document-level context that resolves cross-passage references. We introduce evidence-based generation (EBG), requiring models to return an answer with line-level citations, and a joint Ans+Evid metric that scores correctness and grounding simultaneously. We reformulate two corpora into evidence-grounded multi-document QA benchmarks- MultiHop-RAG and QASPER-MP, a multi-paper adaptation of QASPER with realistic short paper cues, and pair them with a controlled synthetic two-hop haystack and an evidence-augmented NoLiMa spanning 10K-1M characters. Across ten proprietary frontier and three open-weight LLMs, EBG isolates two RAG limitations that long-context models avoid: similarity retrieval surfaces any relevant chunk far more reliably than all required chunks for multi-fact queries, and chunking destroys document meta-information needed to resolve identity-bearing queries. EBG also exposes grounding failures invisible to answer-only evaluation, including incomplete citations in two-needle settings and a widening gap between Ans and Ans+Evid when context contradicts parametric knowledge. Data and code are available at this https URL.

Title: A Multi-granularity Interaction Network for Relational Triple Extraction

  • Authors: Xiaocheng Luo, Yanping Chen, Zaifu Zhan
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26952
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26952
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26952]] A Multi-granularity Interaction Network for Relational Triple Extraction(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26952)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Relational triple extraction identifies entity relations from raw sentences without depending on entity type information. The performance of traditional methods is seriously degenerated by ``unseen'' entities, because these methods can't build contextual dependencies relevant ``unseen'' entities. This paper addresses this challenge by proposing a multi-granularity interaction network that leverages word-word, entity-relation, and head-tail interactions to capture deep semantic dependencies. By incorporating multiple iterations, the multi-granularity interaction network is effective to refine the representations corresponding to head and tail entities. It enhances the global semantic features associated with relational triples, aiding in the remediation of triples that may have been overlooked or omitted in earlier steps. Title: Deep Auxiliary Learning via Bilevel Feature Optimization for Label-Efficient Medical Image Segmentation

  • Authors: Fuping Wu, Xi Jia, Le Zhang, Jiahua Li, Rima Mustafa, Thomas E. Nichols, Bartlomiej W. Papiez

  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Auxiliary learning (AL), which can improve the performance of a primary task by leveraging related auxiliary tasks, is commonly regarded as a special case of multi-task learning. Existing AL approaches typically require manually annotated auxiliary labels, increasing annotation costs, particularly in medical imaging where expert labeling is expensive. In this work, we investigate whether segmentation performance can be improved using auxiliary tasks whose labels are automatically derived from existing segmentation annotations, requiring no additional manual labeling. Title: Dynamic System Inspired Continuous Large Language Model for AI4Science Tasks

  • Authors: Zhaoxin Fan, Zheng Zhu, Yuqing Ma, Faguo Wu, Jian Yang, Wenjun Wu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26959
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26959
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26959]] Dynamic System Inspired Continuous Large Language Model for AI4Science Tasks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26959)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Scientific foundation models increasingly operate on continuous phenomena, yet still use discrete residual computation. We ask whether pretrained residual transformations can support additional latent computation without expanding the backbone. To test this possibility, we introduce Continuum-LM, which reuses these transformations as dynamical operators in a continuous-depth language model. Its hidden states evolve through an ordinary differential equation evaluated by higher-order numerical integration, with solver order and step size controlling computation. Across eight scientific tasks, Continuum-LM trains stably and improves mean exact-match accuracy by 7.77 and 10.96 percentage points over discrete counterparts at the 0.6B and 1.7B scales, respectively. At 0.6B, it also outperforms an Ouro-style recurrent control under matched parameter and residual-function evaluation budgets. Together, these results support extensible computational depth through parameter reuse, identifying continuous hidden-state evolution as a practical design dimension for scientific language models beyond parameter scaling.

Title: Transformers Learn Two Competing Sorting Algorithms, and Only One Length-Generalizes

  • Authors: Khashayar Gatmiry, Nathan W. Henry, Chloe H. Su, Emmanuel Abbe, Samy Bengio, Melanie Weber, Jonathan A. Kelner, Peter L. Bartlett
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26974
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26974
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26974]] Transformers Learn Two Competing Sorting Algorithms, and Only One Length-Generalizes(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26974)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: We show that two-layer transformers trained to near-zero loss on a sorting task converge to one of two mechanistically distinct solution classes depending on their initialization. One solution class consists of solutions that do not use the second attention layer to sort, but also fail to length-generalize. The other, which uses both attention layers, length-generalizes perfectly, despite only being trained to sort sequences of a fixed length. By carefully choosing the weight initialization, we demonstrate that we can steer the model toward learning one solution or the other. We identify input types that are rare at the training length but increasingly common at longer sequence lengths as a source of this disparity in length-generalization, and find that layer-wise specialization of the second class of solutions allows them to handle these rare sequences more accurately. In the second class, we discover a mechanism by which the second layer attention can use the contextual information gathered by the first layer attention to reduce its search space. On the related task of finding the minimum of a sequence, we find a similar two solution-type phenomenon, but with the opposite generalization pattern, where a different form of layer-wise specialization causes the two-layer solution to length-generalize worse than the single-attention solution.

Title: Think Before You Transcribe: Context-Steered Reasoning for Hotword-Aware Speech Recognition

  • Authors: Qihao Wang, Hang Su, Fan Cui, Lichun Fan, Jian Luan, Yue Hu
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26986
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26986
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26986]] Think Before You Transcribe: Context-Steered Reasoning for Hotword-Aware Speech Recognition(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26986)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Contextual automatic speech recognition (ASR) improves rare-entity recognition by supplying candidate hotwords, but retrieved candidates may be incomplete or incorrect. A reliable system must therefore exploit a relevant candidate without allowing unsupported alternatives to override the acoustic evidence. We propose a context-steered reasoning framework that generates a short, audio-grounded caption before transcription. This task-specific representation lets the model assess candidate relevance before making a lexical decision. Supervised fine-tuning establishes the structured caption-to-transcription behavior, while targeted trajectory injection strengthens group relative policy optimization when difficult utterances yield only unsuccessful rollouts. Experiments across diverse context conditions show that the proposed method consistently improves both transcription quality and hotword recognition while remaining robust to irrelevant candidates.

Title: CoASFL: Coupled Consistency Optimization for Asynchronous Split Federated Learning

  • Authors: Junhao Cheng, Yunming Liao, Hongli Xu, Yang Xu, Zuan Xie
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26992
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.26992
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.26992]] CoASFL: Coupled Consistency Optimization for Asynchronous Split Federated Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26992)
  • Keywords: federate
  • Abstract: Split Federated Learning (SFL) has emerged as a promising paradigm for training large-scale deep models on resource-constrained edge devices. Although asynchronous SFL alleviates the straggler bottleneck caused by system heterogeneity, it inevitably introduces two coupled dimensions of inconsistency: horizontal inconsistency across clients and vertical inconsistency between clients and the server. These inconsistencies, further exacerbated by statistical heterogeneity, result in biased activation representations, model divergence, and severe client drift. To address these challenges, we propose CoASFL, an inconsistency-aware asynchronous SFL framework that incorporates a dynamic activation merging mechanism and a consistency-aware model aggregation mechanism. Specifically, CoASFL constructs more consistent activation sequences from heterogeneous clients to alleviate horizontal inconsistency, and dynamically adjusts aggregation weights according to client participation frequencies and progress gaps to mitigate vertical inconsistency. By jointly coordinating activation-level and model-level consistency, CoASFL effectively suppresses inconsistency propagation during asynchronous training. Extensive experiments on an 80-device heterogeneous edge testbed demonstrate that CoASFL consistently outperforms state-of-the-art SFL approaches, accelerating training by 1.61$\times$ to 3.74$\times$ and improving final model accuracy by up to 3.26\% under heterogeneous scenarios.

  • Abstract: Activation steering for ASR systems is an underexplored technique in improving both ASR performance and demographic fairness. Title: Reliable Estimation of Differential Privacy under Selection Bias

  • Keywords: privacy, attack, membership infer
  • Abstract: Statistical privacy audits are essential for verifying the theoretical privacy guarantees of differentially private (DP) mechanisms. Auditors typically use Membership Inference Attacks (MIAs) to estimate a lower bound on the true privacy leakage of DP mechanisms at a specified confidence level, which should not exceed the theoretical privacy upper bound guaranteed by DP. However, we found that many auditing implementations in prior work introduce a selection bias when running MIAs, which leads to statistically invalid and overestimated lower bounds. Because most audits are not tight, this bias has thus far gone unnoticed: the estimated lower bounds remained well below the theoretical upper bounds. This work employs tools from the statistical literature to correct this bias. We present an overview of the different correction methods and assess their respective advantages. Furthermore, we perform a comprehensive re-analysis of results returned by prior audit implementations and show that without statistical correction, these audits violate the theoretical upper bounds up to four times more often than their claimed confidence level implies. With correction applied, the resulting lower bounds are statistically valid, and violations occur at no more than the nominal rate.

  • Abstract: The feature-extraction quality of pre-trained models sets the ceiling of downstream transfer learning, yet few-shot model selection remains difficult: supervised evaluation on scarce labels is unstable and yields fluctuating rankings. EGiME performs $K$-means clustering over all labeled and unlabeled samples, then combines two entropy terms: an inter-cluster entropy measuring global separability, and an intra-cluster entropy measuring the compactness of labeled anchors, fused through an inverse-frequency weight that emphasizes small clusters. Title: Hilbert-Scale Stability of Posterior Diffusion Priors for Ill-Posed Signal Recovery

  • Authors: Bahman Moraffah, Raha Moraffah
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.IT, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27030
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27030
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27030]] Hilbert-Scale Stability of Posterior Diffusion Priors for Ill-Posed Signal Recovery(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27030)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Indirect and noisy observations are difficult to invert when the forward operator smooths out the unknown signal. In this case, the unstable modes are precisely the modes that the data reveals least, thus the success of any reconstruction method depends on how prior information interacts with the geometry of the operator across regularity scales. Diffusion priors have become ubiquitous for such problems; however, the current theory still does not determine when a learned score remains informative under ill-posed forward operators. In this paper, we study posterior diffusion for linear inverse problems on Hilbert scales and derive a stability theory under an explicit score-amplification condition. Under a smoothing condition on the forward map, a source condition on the truth, and weak-norm control of score error, we derive non-asymptotic recovery bounds in \(\Hs^s\) that separate observation noise, score approximation, and reverse-time discretization. These bounds give a recoverable regularity threshold determined jointly by operator smoothing, truth regularity, score regularity, and the posterior correction. The threshold specifies the Hilbert-scale norms \(\Hs^s\) in which weak-norm score error remains controlled after propagation through the posterior reverse dynamics. For larger regularity indices \(s\), the inverse geometry magnifies the same error, and the Hilbert-scale recovery estimate no longer gives uniform stability. We also prove that a posterior correction adapted to the forward operator improves stability relative to isotropic guidance in the regularity ranges where the smoothing action of the forward map matters most. The recovery estimates give a regularity-sensitive theory of posterior diffusion and show how learned score error, operator smoothing, and reconstruction norm jointly determine the stable range predicted by the analysis.

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: When given an image and a text prompt, vision-language models (VLMs) can produce seemingly sophisticated text responses. However, they are also prone to hallucinate objects in their output, which can have detrimental consequences in safety-critical applications. To detect these hallucinations, certain methods interestingly leverage internal signals from the VLM as it generates the text response; potentially, these methods use the same signals that led to a hallucination to detect it. We conduct an in-depth analysis to understand the robustness of these detection methods. First, we show that a naive baseline of a generated word's token position in the VLM text response achieves competitive performance. Further, we reveal how some detection methods rely on token position and use this heuristic as a shortcut. Second, we explore detection methods' sensitivity to language and vision modalities. Lastly, we demonstrate that different detection methods can carry complementary signals; when ensembling methods together, adding weaker methods to an ensemble of higher performing ones still offers performance improvements. Altogether, while there may be useful internal VLM signals to detect hallucinations, our investigation reveals important limitations of the current methods that try to leverage them.

Title: ClockMark: Adaptive and Host-Dependent Watermarking for Large Language Models

  • Authors: Yupeng Qiu, Han Fang, Ee-Chien Chang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27079
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27079
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27079]] ClockMark: Adaptive and Host-Dependent Watermarking for Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27079)
  • Keywords: robust, watermark, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language model (LLM) watermarking operates on the next-token distribution that evolves during generation. In streaming generation, each sampled token becomes part of the text and affects subsequent distributions. Embedding therefore proceeds with a changing host, unlike classical watermarking, which adapts embedding to an existing host signal. To accommodate this difference, we formulate a novel embedding framework for LLM watermarking based on two principles. We first argue that embedding should be adaptive, retaining the state of previous sampling decisions to better guide subsequent choices. We further argue that embedding should be host-dependent, using the current LLM distribution to constrain both token selection and state updates. Guided by these principles and the two requirements of distribution preservation and robustness to text editing, we propose ClockMark. It combines adaptive and host-dependent embedding with message spreading, embedding the full payload in each watermarked token. Theoretically, we prove that ClockMark preserves the original LLM sequence distribution, thereby maintaining generation quality. Empirically, extensive experiments against 12 baselines demonstrate improved robustness to text editing, with gains of up to 16.08 percentage points in bit accuracy over the strongest tested baseline.

Across all four major types of adversarial attacks with four widely-deployed on-device models, BMO achieves an average success rate of 68.68% in defense, far exceeding the most competitive baseline, which attains only 22.32% in defending against cross-node attacks. Title: AutoRedClaw: Automated Red-Teaming for Claw-Style Agents

  • Authors: Hao Li, Tongyan Hu, Weidi Luo, Shanghao Shi, Xiaogeng Liu, Aiden Suh, Yulong Cao, Ning Zhang, William Yeoh, Yevgeniy Vorobeychik, Chaowei Xiao

Once poisoned, this persistent state is repeatedly reloaded into the agent's context, creating persistent threats across tasks and sessions. Existing security evaluation systems struggle to evaluate these complex claw-style systems since they rely heavily on manually constructed test cases and environments, which limits their scale and coverage; in addition, these manually designed cases quickly become outdated as agents evolve, and their evaluation settings leave a gap with how claw-style agents are deployed in practice. AutoRedClaw achieves an effective attack rate above 30% at less than $0.10 per successful sample. From its outputs, we construct RedClaw-Bench, a 330-case benchmark that yields substantially higher attack success rates (ASRs) than AgentDojo, AgentDyn, and DTap. Across seven harnesses and four frontier models, most agent systems exceed 50% ASR, showing that our red-teaming pipeline provides a strong evaluation signal even for the most advanced agent systems. Our code and benchmark are released at this https URL.

Title: Target-Aware Curation of Hallucination Stress Tests for Large Language Models

  • Authors: Jiho Lee, Bingquan Zhang, Lin Ting Liang, Soroush Vosoughi, Weicheng Ma
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI

  • Abstract: Hallucination stress tests can yield similar average scores across language models even when different prompts elicit hallucinations in each model. Title: When Pebbles Are Not Atomic: A Polynomial Separation Between Pure and Entangled Pebbling

  • Authors: Piotr Marszalik
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27249
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27249
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27249]] When Pebbles Are Not Atomic: A Polynomial Separation Between Pure and Entangled Pebbling(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27249)
  • Keywords: secure
  • Abstract: Graph pebbling is the model in which the memory cost of memory-hard functions and proofs of space is measured, and pebbling lower bounds are the main way to argue that such constructions are secure. It has long seemed that the model is faithful to reality. It is known that it is not exact: an adversary who stores encoded information instead of whole values can do better, but so far only by small factors: a factor of 1.5 in the work that introduced entangled pebbling (Alwen, Chen, Kamath, Kolmogorov, Pietrzak and Tessaro) and, recently, a factor of about log n/log log n (de Rezende, Engström and Reyzin). One could still hope that pebbling is right up to such small factors. In pure pebbling, memory consists of whole labels; entangled pebbling also allows encoded state, such as XORs of labels. We show that the two models can differ polynomially. We study the setting in which proofs of space and data-dependent memory-hard functions are used: a state is prepared in advance, a random challenge has to be answered from it, and memory is paid for as long as it is held. Our graphs are explicit and have indegree two, and on them a pure adversary can do no better, up to a constant factor, than storing every possible answer in advance. Still, an adversary who keeps XORs instead of labels pays polynomially less. This ends the hope that pure pebbling is a faithful model of memory up to small factors. The heart of the paper is a simple combinatorial game, together with a special class of graphs found by Alon, Moitra and Sudakov.

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27259

  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27259]] Guided Diffusion for Trustworthy Synthetic Text Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27259)

  • Abstract: The rapid evolution of Large Language Models is fueled by massive datasets, yet this reliance poses severe privacy risks, as models can inadvertently memorize and even directly output sensitive training data. In this paper, we propose a new paradigm for private synthetic text generation: Guided Diffusion. Leveraging the unique steerability of Diffusion Language Models, we introduce novel sampling and re-masking procedures that actively guide the iterative denoising steps. We further extend the framework to Data-Specific Indistinguishability (DSI), which adds a formal certificate against data poisoning and memorization at a small utility cost.

We therefore study saliency-guided augmentation as an explanation-aware strategy for low-resource HCR. In some settings, saliency-constrained training improves explanation consistency even when predictive performance is similar. This shows that predictive metrics alone can obscure differences in model sensitivity under perturbation, motivating explanation consistency as a complementary evaluation dimension.

  • Abstract: Deep latent variable models (DLVMs) underpin many modern analysis pipelines, for example in single-cell genomics, yet their prior distributions have received markedly little attention. Here, we identify two key limitations of the VampPrior: it ties prior and posterior uncertainty to a single amortized covariance and clusters poorly due to its fixed uniform mixture weights. We unleash the VampPrior by decoupling its prior and posterior covariances, resolving the conflict of generating high prior and low posterior entropy with the same shared parameters and substantially improving model evidence.   • Abstract: This article presents a Distributed Key Management System (DKMS) that provisions quantum-safe symmetric encryption keys to applications across a network.   • Authors: Leo Elmecker-Plakolm, Lukasz Szpruch, Avishek Joey Bose, Matthew Wicker

Yet current practice in assessing adversarial robustness is restricted to individual robustness scores and attack transfer rates, leaving open the unexplored fundamental question: how many attacks suffice for catastrophic widespread compromise? In this paper, we investigate the empirical nature of shared vulnerabilities within a model pool and introduce Systemic Adversarial Robustness (SAR), a novel measure of shared vulnerability of a model pool through the attack diversity needed to exploit it. We empirically test the shared vulnerability of vision and large language models and characterize their shared collective vulnerability curve.   • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27357

  • Abstract: Byzantine-robust aggregation shields federated learning from malicious updates, but the rules that filter out suspect updates do so by comparing clients' parameter vectors. We propose LiBRA, which compares clients on what their models compute rather than on the parameters that compute them, using a fingerprint of $C^2$ values for $C$ classes, the class-conditional logit response of each client's model to pseudo-patterns optimised against it. The variants thus match the robustness of the rules they extend, while their trust decision reads no client parameter, compares up to 49,090 times fewer values, and can by construction compare clients whose architectures differ, at the cost of one pattern optimisation per client per round.

Title: PerQ: Inverse Generative Modeling for Neural Image Compression via Quantization Error Compensation

  • Authors: Tianhao Peng, Ho Man Kwan, Fan Zhang, Shan Liu, Dave Bull
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27374
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27374
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27374]] PerQ: Inverse Generative Modeling for Neural Image Compression via Quantization Error Compensation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27374)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Neural image codecs face a fundamental trade-off between pixel fidelity and perceptual quality. Distortion-optimized codecs preserve pixel-wise fidelity but produce blurred reconstructions at low bitrates, whereas generative codecs leverage learned priors to improve perceptual quality but tend to exhibit saturating PSNR at higher bitrates and require separate models for different bitrate targets. We propose PerQ, which reframes perceptual reconstruction as inverse generative modeling with bounded support. Specifically, we define the quantization cell as the set of latents that yield the same transmitted symbols and derive a transport bound showing that reconstructions from this cell remain within a rate-determined radius of the codec output. This motivates performing generative reconstruction within the codec-induced quantization cell, rather than sampling from an unconstrained image prior. PerQ implements this principle by attaching a rate-conditioned flow-matching compensator to a frozen, multi-rate distortion-optimized backbone, enabling perceptual enhancement while preserving the backbone's coding performance. Experiments on Kodak and CLIC2020 show that PerQ achieves perceptual performance comparable to generative codecs such as MS-ILLM in LPIPS and FID, while maintaining competitive PSNR relative to distortion-optimized codecs such as ELIC. A single model checkpoint supports a wide bitrate range with fast decoding, and the compensator provides consistent gains across three different backbones. The implementation is available at this https URL.

We explore rank-reducing transformations based on the recently introduced J-space corresponding to the averaged Jacobian from the current layer to the LLM output, projecting activations onto the ``live'' subspace spanned by eigenmodes of the Jacobian with larger singular values (which we expect to be more semantically relevant). The promise of shaping is demonstrated via evaluations against dense activations and state-of-the-art SAEs on benchmarks including SAEBench and SAE Probes.

  • Authors: Shreyas Kulkarni, Uttam Gurram, Madhav Kanda, Krishna Teja Chitty Venkata, Shichu Chen, Kartik Balasubramaniam, Supriyo Chakraborty, Sambit Sahu
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27379

  • Keywords: diffusion, large language model
  • Abstract: Block-diffusion speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference by drafting multiple tokens in parallel. However, one-step drafting in discrete diffusion is limited by token factorization error: each token is predicted from the same masked block, without conditioning on the tokens selected at other positions in that step. This can produce inconsistent drafts and reduce acceptance length. Iterative refinement mitigates this limitation by conditioning later predictions on previously committed tokens, though additional passes increase drafting cost. To address this trade-off between draft fidelity and inference latency, we introduce DStill, a two-stage framework that distills a target-conditioned drafter's own multi-step refinement into one-step drafting. Using a curriculum that gradually transitions from partial to fully masked inputs, we successfully transfer the multi-step refinement capabilities into one step. Across reasoning, math, code benchmarks on three model families DStill improves acceptance length by up to 28% and speedup by up to 25% over one-step DFlash, achieving up to 6.45x speedup over auto-regressive decoding.

Title: AgenticVulHunter: Bringing Local Agentic Vulnerability Hunting to Your Terminal

Title: Sample-efficient learning with Dense Associative Memories

  • Authors: Andrew Lucas, Dmitry Krotov
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27384
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27384
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27384]] Sample-efficient learning with Dense Associative Memories(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27384)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: We analytically study the evolution of the energy function of Dense Associative Memories (DenseAMs) as they train on randomly sampled codewords of random expander low-density parity-check codes (a.k.a. solutions of random XORSAT problems). We identify two distinct regimes and a transition between them, reminiscent of grokking in neural networks. A regime of small training set size is where the model memorizes a few training examples but cannot discover the underlying rules of the entire codeword dictionary generative process. The opposite regime, corresponding to a larger training set size, is where the model has uncovered the sparse constraints that generated the entire data set after seeing only polynomially many patterns. We also design a novel class of DenseAMs with a thresholding learning rule, and show that any amount of thresholding enables the model to perform simplified sparse Hamiltonian learning, which exponentially reduces the number of training examples needed to learn the true underlying data set. In this regime, the model learns how to discover $\exp[\mathrm{O}(n)]$ solutions from only $\mathrm{O}(\log n)$ training samples, which is asymptotically optimal for any learning algorithm. When training on codewords of certain random codes, the model can even learn the underlying code when a small O(1) fraction of bits are latent variables. These latent variables are not shown to the model during learning, but will be discovered by sparsifying observed higher-order correlations. For certain random codes, we show that a simple and conservative algorithm learns a minimal latent representation for the data set. This provides a transparent, physics-motivated, and analytically tractable toy model for how deep latent representations emerge in an associative memory training.

Title: TokenFlow: Latent Token Rectified Flow Matching for 3D Point Cloud Generation

  • Authors: Linhao Wang, Dongjin Huang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27418
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27418

  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: 3D point clouds generation requires capturing both part-level layout and surface detail while preserving permutation equivariance. Existing latent generative models often encode an entire shape into a single vector, which limits their ability to retain part-level structure. We propose TokenFlow, a hierarchical flow matching framework that represents each shape as a set of latent tokens and couples a rectified flow over these tokens with a conditional flow over points. A bottleneck of $M \ll N$ learnable tokens aggregates global context through cross-attention at $O(NM)$ cost. On ShapeNet, TokenFlow achieves the best MMD and coverage across airplane, car, and chair, while obtaining 1-NNA comparable to the strongest baselines. On ModelNet, its learned latent set reaches the best reported linear-evaluation accuracy on ModelNet10. These results show that representing a shape as a set of latent tokens, aggregated at $O(NM)$ cost, provides an effective representation for 3D point cloud generation.

Title: Scaling Graph Reasoning in Language Models with Learnable Graph Positional Encodings

  • Abstract: Solving reasoning tasks supported on graphs typically requires providing a Language Reasoning Model (LRM) with a serialized description of the graph. Furthermore, even when graphs fit in context, performance sharply decays as the graphs grow. We propose to address this by augmenting LRMs with scalable and permutation equivariant graph positional encodings that modulate attention logits. Title: Learning-Theoretic Foundation for General Coded Computing: The Adversarial Setting

  • Authors: Parsa Moradi, Hanzaleh Akbarinodehi, Mohammad Ali Maddah-Ali
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.DC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27440
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27440
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27440]] Learning-Theoretic Foundation for General Coded Computing: The Adversarial Setting(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27440)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Adversarial workers pose a severe challenge in distributed computing systems by returning corrupted outputs that can substantially distort the recovered computations. Coded computing addresses this threat by introducing redundancy across worker assignments. Existing exact and approximate coded-computing methods typically handle adversarial outputs by explicitly locating and correcting or removing erroneous evaluations, which adds significant computational overhead at the master node. Moreover, existing analyses do not characterize how the recovery error scales with the number of workers and the adversarial budget. In this paper, we develop an adversarially robust extension of general coded computing (GCC), a learning-theoretic approximate coded-computing framework that designs the encoder and decoder by minimizing an upper bound on the end-to-end recovery error. Rather than identifying corrupted workers, the proposed method uses smoothness regularization to limit the influence of their outputs. The resulting encoder and decoder can be implemented using smoothing splines with linear master-side complexity. We derive a non-asymptotic bound on the worst-case end-to-end approximation error by decomposing it into three components: (1) the encoder approximation error, (2) the decoder reconstruction error under fully honest results, and (3) the adversarial perturbation error. For maximum of $q\geq 1$ adversarial workers among $N$ workers results in a worst-case error of order $\mathcal{O}((q/N)^{6/5})$ with appropriate selection of smoothness parameter, demonstrating that the approximation error vanishes asymptotically whenever the number of adversaries grows sublinearly with the network size. Experiments on scalar computation and neural-network inference are consistent with the predicted convergence behavior.

Title: LoRASpace: A Pool-Wide Shared Substrate for Static and Dynamic Multi-LoRA Composition

  • Authors: Thamirawaran Sathiyalogeswaran, Navindu De Silva, Chamikara Siriwardane, W. K. Alwis, Jason Mars, Udaya Sampath K. Perera Miriya Thanthrige, Ranga Rodrigo
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27444
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27444
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27444]] LoRASpace: A Pool-Wide Shared Substrate for Static and Dynamic Multi-LoRA Composition(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27444)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) attaches small low-rank matrices to a frozen backbone, and public repositories host many LoRAs per backbone, each for a different concept or task. Combining several LoRAs in one generation forces a trade-off. Merging their weights into one update needs a single forward pass, but LoRAs trained for different concepts interfere and quality drops. Running each LoRA in its own forward pass (composite) preserves quality, but inference time grows linearly with the number of active LoRAs. We study a property of LoRA pools overlooked by prior multi-LoRA work. In real LoRA pools, the stacked low-rank factors need far fewer directions than their summed ranks. Using singular value decomposition, we find the minimal subspace that keeps 99% of the factor energy. On three diffusion pools, only 46% to 57% of the summed LoRA ranks is needed, and the subspace grows sub-linearly with pool size. We build on this overlap with LoRASpace, a training-free method that computes the subspace once per pool and reuses it in two inference modes, a static mode at merge cost and a dynamic mode for input-dependent routing. On the diffusion pools, static LoRASpace matches composite quality at merge cost, and dynamic LoRASpace is more than 6x faster than composite with six active LoRAs on a 17-LoRA pool, mainly because it runs one backbone pass instead of six. In an ablation, static variants at the same rank match LoRASpace, so the quality gain does not depend on the pool-wide basis. On the near-orthogonal LoRA-Retriever benchmark, compression is small and gains come from coupling with external retrieval.

  • Abstract: A controller can guide a language model toward safe and useful responses by choosing when to assist, ask for clarification, or refuse. We study decision sufficiency: whether the best controller using a representation can match the minimum average loss attainable with full state information, under the same strategies and evaluation criteria. For interfaces with finitely many outputs, we compute the optimal loss exactly and separate the three sources of excess loss. Pairwise checks can miss information loss: states with the same controller input may share an optimal strategy in every pair but not across the whole group.   • Authors: Niccolò Ronchetti

  • Abstract: Mitigating catastrophic risks from large language models requires excising hazardous operational capabilities, such as cyberwarfare exploitation and pathogen enhancement, without degrading benign scientific utility. In this work, we present Orthogonal Boundary Unlearning (OBU), a mathematically grounded framework designed to eliminate collateral damage on benign dual-use capabilities. This guarantees that benign representations experience zero parameter perturbation along the spanned calibration subspace. We present two complementary realizations of OBU: Projected LoRA, which applies the orthogonal constraint during iterative gradient-based fine-tuning, and Closed-Form Weight Surgery, which directly edits model weights in a single analytical step without training. Across four modern open-weight foundation models (Gemma-3-4B, Qwen3.5-4B, SmolLM3-3B, and Granite-4.2-3B), OBU achieves strong dual-use concept separation, substantially mitigates in-context priming attacks, and demonstrates durable resistance against adversarial relearning without degrading benign domain competence.

Title: Adversarial Benchmarks and BenchDoor Attacks: Exploiting Benchmark Incentives for Backdoor Attacks

  • Authors: Yixin Wu, Rachel Hong, Jamie Morgenstern, Tadayoshi Kohno
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27533

  • Abstract: Machine learning benchmarks act as guideposts for model development and spur the creation of training datasets intended to improve benchmark scores. The adversary either manipulates a benchmark's training or test data, creating an adversarial benchmark, or supplies an independent training dataset while leaving the benchmark unchanged.   • Abstract: Zero-shot composed image retrieval (ZS-CIR) aims to retrieve a target image based on a reference image and modification text. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of the proposed framework for the ZS-CIR. Our code is available at: this https URL.

Title: KNOX: Hardware-assisted Regulatory Sandbox for Generative Models

  • Authors: Vasisht Duddu, Adam Caulfield, Xi He
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27543
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27543
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27543]] KNOX: Hardware-assisted Regulatory Sandbox for Generative Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27543)
  • Keywords: protect, defense, generative
  • Abstract: Regulations require companies to run AI models in a regulatory sandbox, an isolated environment that enables monitoring model behavior by regulators, prior to deployment. But, they do not spell out the technical mechanisms to design such a sandbox. Ideally, a regulatory sandbox should show (i) compliance (checks against regulations, and deviations from intended behavior, i.e., unauthorized tool and system calls); (ii) confidentiality (protects model weights from regulators); (iii) attestability (produces tamper-resistant evidence of model behavior for monitoring); and (iv) scalability (evidence is verifiable by multiple regulators). Several prior works (information-flow control, prompt-injection defenses, control-flow integrity, and traditional sandboxing) address specific compliance aspects but lack confidentiality, attestability, and scalability. TEEs meet some of these requirements, but simply running models inside a TEE is insufficient as we need compliance mechanisms. We present KNOX, the first regulatory sandbox, built by running AI workloads inside TEEs. We demonstrate KNOX using agentic workloads. For compliance, we derive an attested policy manifest (with the applicable regulations, the expected tool calls, and the permitted system calls) for a given user prompt. Before an executor performs an action, an attested reference monitor compares it against the attested policy manifest and allows it only if it matches. Every action and verdict is recorded in an attested execution graph, enabling regulators to verify the model's execution and its compliance. We show that KNOX meets all requirements, and can check for compliance with regulations and deviations from intended behavior (e.g., due to prompt injection); and incurs low overhead (<1%).

Title: Can Native Safety Travel? Reusing Safety Pretraining Across Models via On-Policy Distillation

  • Authors: Bingqi Shang, Yang Zhang, Yiwei Chen, Changsheng Wang, Shiyu Chang, Bingquan Shen, Sijia Liu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27555
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27555
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27555]] Can Native Safety Travel? Reusing Safety Pretraining Across Models via On-Policy Distillation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27555)
  • Keywords: attack, robust
  • Abstract: Safety pretraining has emerged as a promising paradigm for embedding safety deeply into a model during pretraining, yielding robustness to subsequent fine-tuning that conventional safety post-training often lacks. We refer to this safety acquired during model pretraining as native safety. Yet native safety is expensive to acquire, confined to the model in which it was pretrained, and often accompanied by lower general utility. In this work, we rethink the practical value of safety-pretrained models and introduce, for the first time, the native safety reuse problem: Can native safety be transferred from a specialized, potentially smaller, safety-pretrained "teacher" to standard-pretrained "students," enabling them to inherit its robustness while preserving their general utility? We show that native safety can "travel" through on-policy distillation. A standard-pretrained student generates responses to safety-pretraining prompts, while a small safety-pretrained teacher supervises the student's token-level predictions along its own generated trajectories; cross-tokenizer alignment further enables transfer across model families. The resulting students exhibit substantially greater safety robustness than their safety-post-trained counterparts while retaining higher general utility than the teacher: subsequent fine-tuning raises the average attack success rate (ASR) of the 1.7B student by only 5.00%, compared with 11.58% for safety post-training, and its average utility of 48.20% far exceeds the aligned teacher's 27.42%. Unlike conventional large-to-small knowledge distillation, our framework enables selective, small-to-large safety transfer: it reuses the robust safety expertise of a smaller pretrained teacher without inheriting its utility limitations. These findings establish native safety as a reusable model property rather than one permanently tied to the originally pretrained model.

Title: Two Is Enough: Binary Classification Priors Suffice for Multi-Class In-Context Tabular Prediction

  • Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) pretrain a transformer on large-scale synthetic priors so that a single forward pass yields zero-shot tabular prediction. Mechanistically, classification in TabICLv2 is carried not by a fixed-cardinality output head but by an in-context comparison operator matching query-row embeddings against class-conditional embeddings, so the cardinality of the output space is set by the labels present in the context, not by the label space seen during pretraining. This makes a binary prior structurally cheaper to generate and cache than a ten-class prior, demotes "number of classes" from an architectural constraint to a tunable hyperparameter, and points to a class-count-agnostic regime of TFM pretraining.

Title: Factual Access States in Diffusion Language Models: From Mechanism to Knowledge Editing and Retrieval

  • Authors: Yuchen Guo, Zhuoyuan Mao, Yiwen Guo
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.IR, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27580
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27580
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27580]] Factual Access States in Diffusion Language Models: From Mechanism to Knowledge Editing and Retrieval(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27580)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Factual recall is a lookup: some representation inside the network serves as the key, and what that key is made of decides which queries can retrieve the fact, what an edit must cover, and what a retriever can read. We call it the factual access state. In autoregressive (AR) models the subject position enriches attributes largely independently of the relation, and relation-specific selection happens later, at the readout position. In masked diffusion language models (DLMs) we find the state is assembled differently, and much earlier: in the first attention layer the wording of the relation is routed into the position of whichever entity is visible and the early feed-forward layers look the fact up on this fused representation. The state therefore varies with the relation's surface form and with the query direction, so the queries about one fact occupy a region of state space rather than a point, and an operation on the fact succeeds only for queries that enter it. We treat the state as an interface to a DLM's factual knowledge, and put it to two uses. Writing to the state gives a knowledge editor: from one declarative statement, Multi-View Edit fits one shared value per direction across that region and installs it in the early-MLP block, preserving source-prompt reliability while substantially improving paraphrase and reverse question answering, where AR editors gain near zero. Reading it suggests a complementary use: the state at each entity position can serve as a relation-specific retrieval vector with no training, returning both pieces of evidence for two-entity queries on which pooled, prompted and lexical retrievers almost never return both.

  • Abstract: Multispectral iris presentation attack detection benefits from observing the same iris across multiple near-infrared wavelengths, but existing methods often use wavelength information mainly for conditioning or fusion. Title: Big Secrets Small Shares: Revisiting Secure Multi-Party Computation

  • Authors: Wilton Kim, Van Long Phuoc Pham, Stanislav Kruglik, Han Mao Kiah
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.IT
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27609
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27609
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27609]] Big Secrets Small Shares: Revisiting Secure Multi-Party Computation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27609)
  • Keywords: secure, security
  • Abstract: At CRYPTO 2007, Damgård and Nielsen introduced a preprocessing approach to multi-party computation (MPC) to achieve linear communication complexity. Another approach to reduce communication complexity was proposed by Cramer, Damgård, and de Haan at EUROCRYPT 2007, which involved using ramp-secret sharing schemes to jointly share base-field components of secrets from the extension field. In this paper, we combine these two ideas to achieve a non-asymptotic reduction of communication cost in a multi-phase MPC setup. We introduce a new modification of the ramp secret sharing scheme, called compact sharing, and tailor the MPC protocol to accommodate this modification. Compact sharing masks the polynomial coefficients that would otherwise leak during degree reduction. The protocol provides perfect security against static semi-honest adversaries under an explicit tradeoff between the corruption threshold and the extension degree. When the extension degree scales linearly with the number of parties, the gate-evaluation core achieves constant communication overhead relative to an extension-field element, amortized over full preprocessing batches; input and output communication is counted separately.

Title: DISTA-LP: Privacy-Preserving Distributed Linear Programming for Metric Differential Privacy

  • Authors: Michael Oluwole, Ruiyao Liu, Aleksander Stevens, Chenxi Qiu
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27615
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27615
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27615]] DISTA-LP: Privacy-Preserving Distributed Linear Programming for Metric Differential Privacy(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27615)
  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: Linear-programming (LP) mechanisms for metric differential privacy (mDP) enable fine-grained, task-aware utility control, but they are computationally expensive and become privacy-fragile in distributed settings: uploading user-specific LP coefficient matrices (e.g., distance and loss blocks) to a central solver can itself leak sensitive information. We present DISTA-LP, a privacy-preserving distributed mDP optimization framework that replaces raw coefficient uploads with compact surrogate parameterizations and releases only calibrated, perturbed parameter vectors, thereby reducing both upload-stage leakage and problem dimensionality. On the server side, DISTA-LP reconstructs approximate coefficients and solves a scalable mastersubproblem formulation via Benders decomposition. To certify end-to-end privacy, we introduce a probabilistic mDP (PmDP) auditor that empirically quantifies the privacy loss induced by parameter release, allocates the required privacy budget prior to optimization, and composes it with the downstream mechanism to obtain an end-to-end mDP guarantee. Experiments on large realworld road-network datasets (Rome, New York City, and London) show that DISTA-LP achieves near-zero mDP violation rates, substantially reduces computation time compared to monolithic and hybrid baselines, and matches or improves utility, thereby demonstrating stronger upload privacy and significantly improved scalability.

  • Keywords: robust, diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Temperature scaling is typically applied as a post-hoc transformation of model probabilities. In posterior-induced flow language models, this creates two mismatches. First, posterior tempering implicitly changes both the endpoint distribution and the distribution of intermediate noisy states, while the denoiser remains trained on the original states. Second, the tempered posterior weights are combined with transport dynamics derived for the untempered flow. These mismatches are reflected empirically: lowering temperature improves GSM8K performance much less reliably in flow LMs than in discrete diffusion LMs. We introduce Bayes-Consistent Tempering (BCT), which makes temperature explicit in both the distributions and dynamics of the generative flow. Its learned realization trains the denoiser on tempered intermediate states and tempers the endpoint distribution using the corresponding tempered dynamics. We also derive training-free variants for conventionally trained checkpoints. Across hyperspherical flow LMs with two denoiser architectures and a von Mises--Fisher flow LM, BCT improves GSM8K generation across broad temperature ranges and achieves the strongest tempering performance. The gains also extend to Sudoku, demonstrating benefits on tightly constrained generation. At very low temperatures, the learned realization is also markedly more robust than the training-free variants, supporting temperature as a property of the generative flow across training and inference rather than a post-hoc probability adjustment.   • Authors: Yang Liu, Masahiro Kaneko, Chenhui Chu
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27648
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27648

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Recent work reports that the distribution verbalized directly in the output text aligns far better with human opinion distributions than the next-token log-probability distribution of LLMs. However, the verbalized distribution reflects what LLMs know about a group's opinions (knowledge distribution), while what they actually output at deployment is governed by their log-probability distribution (behavior distribution). We find that when prompting LLMs to predict the opinion of a specific group, the most likely answer under the verbalized distribution differs from that under the log-probability distribution for 37% of World Values Survey questions, averaged over three LLMs. Thus, LLMs' knowledge does not match their behavior. The alignment scores for these two distributions reflect only the model's behavior or its knowledge, separately. We therefore propose a learnable combination of the two distributions that follows the most likely answer of the behavior distribution while preserving the shape of the knowledge distribution, with a single hyperparameter trading off behavioral fidelity against distribution quality. Our experiments on three opinion survey datasets and six models show that the balanced distribution improves behavioral consistency over the verbalized distribution from 71.6% to 95.6% on average, while the total variation distance to the human opinion distributions rises only slightly from 28.8% to 33.8%, and it remains effective under temperature scaling and cross-dataset settings.

  • Abstract: Watermarking has emerged as a key tool for identifying text generated by large language models (LLMs), with current schemes embedding statistical signals into the generation process to enable later detection. We show that these schemes exhibit a fundamental link between their detection scores and the token probability shifts they introduce during generation.
