We prove that the resulting attack is optimal across all covered watermark families, and show that the weakness is intrinsic: any watermark designed to maximize statistical detection power necessarily induces the very structure that attackers can exploit. We evaluate our attack on seven popular generative LLM watermarking schemes, three LLMs, and tasks, which reduce detection rates to at most 0.05 across all settings while preserving text quality. Our findings show that current watermarking methods can be reliably defeated under realistic attack conditions, highlighting the need for fundamentally different watermarking designs that decouple detection scores from token probability shifts.

This creates a trade-off between retaining long-term history and adapting to recent changes. We introduce Hybrid Momentum (HyM), which combines a linearly weighted average of past gradients with a geometrically discounted fast momentum. This hybrid construction is modular and can be combined with adaptive preconditioning and other update transformations. In particular, we propose HyMUON, which applies Newton--Schulz orthogonalization to the hybrid direction. Illustrative examples and controlled experiments demonstrate the benefits of combining gradient information across these two timescales. Across image classification, machine translation, and language modeling, HyM and HyMUON match or improve upon strong baselines. HyMUON improves over Muon on IWSLT14, and in both the 100k- and 600k-step GPT-2 124M OpenWebText regimes. For LLaMA-1.3B pretraining on C4, HyMUON achieves a final validation loss of 2.777, compared with 2.903 for Muon.

Overall, these findings show that target-domain success alone is insufficient and highlight cross-dimensional retention as a key criterion for targeted VLA adaptation.

Title: Privacy Accounting for Empirical Denoisers

  • Authors: William Cheng, Mijung Park, Dongjun Kim
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CR, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27710
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27710

  • Keywords: privacy, diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Diffusion models have emerged as the foundation of modern generative systems, yet their high memorization capacity raises privacy concerns. While differentially private (DP) training provides formal guarantees, it comes at a cost in sample quality. In this work, we study a complementary question: how much privacy loss is incurred during the sampling process itself? Directly analyzing modern neural denoisers is challenging because their sensitivity to a single training example is generally intractable. To overcome this challenge, we construct and analyze a clipped privacy-accountable empirical denoiser, a training-free estimator of the Bayes-optimal denoiser, whose per-step sensitivity can be computed explicitly, allowing each denoising step to be a Gaussian mechanism. This perspective enables privacy accounting under Gaussian DP when each training example contributes to each denoising step, and under $f$-DP trade-off functions when each generated sample uses a single randomly subsampled block of the training data. We report every $(\varepsilon, \delta)$ guarantee as a certified upper bound from a privacy loss distribution accountant, and use a cumulant-based Edgeworth approximation as a fast diagnostic, characterizing where it is reliable and where it becomes anti-conservative. We also analyze the privacy loss along the denoising trajectory and show that it grows steeply near the critical window in which semantic content is formed. This observation motivates hybrid samplers that use the private empirical denoiser through the formative steps where it remains a faithful proxy for the neural denoiser, then switch to a public neural denoiser for the remaining refinement. Our results on CIFAR-10 and CelebA-64 show that the privacy of diffusion models depends not only on the training procedure, but also on sampling-time design choices such as subsampling, stopping time, and hybrid denoising.

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27720
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27720

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models can achieve strong Text-to-SQL benchmark performance while still producing schema and query-logic errors. Most evaluations characterize such failures only in the final generated query, making it difficult to determine whether a related error was also observed in an earlier, simpler step or appears only at the final stage. We present a drill-down evaluation framework that decomposes Text-to-SQL queries into progressively more complex executable sub-queries and corresponding natural-language questions, allowing rule-based hallucination categories to be examined across a generation path. We distinguish recurrent hallucinations, in which the same rule-based category is observed in an earlier step and again in the final query, from emergent hallucinations, in which the category is observed at the final step without an earlier detection in the same path. Across five LLMs evaluated on an expanded BIRD-mini benchmark, several categories show appreciable recurrence probabilities, while clause abuse shows comparatively higher conditional emergence. Under the conversational-history condition, recurrence probabilities are generally higher and emergence probabilities are generally lower. These observations indicate that the two conditional measures do not move uniformly in the same direction under additional context and motivate trajectory-level analysis in addition to final-output evaluation.   • Abstract: Guided Imagery (GI) is a mind-based relaxation technique widely used in clinical and therapeutic settings. Anterior cortical regions are identified as the most generalisable neural markers of hypnotic susceptibility.

  • Abstract: Language-model attackers consume computational resources as they explore and interact with a target, creating an opportunity for cyber deception to impose costs. Across 330 sessions, eight model pairs, two attacker profiles and three counter-injection levels, both sides run on identical GPUs and their energy is measured above idle. We use these findings to motivate a proposed SCADA deception architecture with private physical state per identified adversary and deterministic protocol and process emulation.

  • Authors: An-Sheng Jhang

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as judgment assistants and as stand-in participants in psychology experiments, yet little is known about how much their probability estimates move in response to a single new piece of information. Reporting lowers s in all seven point estimates (significantly in six, by 2-5 points) and raises the share of reverse movements in all seven: the effect is consistent but small. Five weighted estimates lower the share of responses at $\leq$5% or $\geq$95% in all seven, but in the two local models they also reduce s by 43% and 68%, whereas in the harnessed configurations the point estimates of the change lie between -3.7 and +0.5 points. An elicitation format that reduces extreme values thus need not preserve sensitivity to evidence; the two should be tested separately. Because $\delta$-NLI annotates only direction, we make no claim about how large an update should be. Code, data and the frozen plan: this https URL

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: In diffusion models, proper distributional learning entails reversing a diffusion process over underlying density functions, whereas a key failure is memorization, in which the reverse process instead learns diffusion from individual training samples. Title: PixelSR: Pixel-Space Orthogonal Flow Decoupling for Real-World Image Super-Resolution

  • Authors: Xiang Lv, Kai Zhao, Qiang Wang, Qiao Mo, Chao Zhou, Mingwen Shao
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27759
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27759
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27759]] PixelSR: Pixel-Space Orthogonal Flow Decoupling for Real-World Image Super-Resolution(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27759)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Latent diffusion models have recently achieved remarkable progress in Real-world Image Super-Resolution (Real-SR). However, existing methods consistently exhibit a persistent seesaw effect: enhancing perceptual realism compromises structural fidelity. This effect, we argue, is not intrinsic to diffusion models, but largely stems from the architectural constraints of prevailing two-stage frameworks. To address this challenge, we propose PixelSR, a novel single-stage Pixel Diffusion Transformer for Real-SR that operates directly in pixel space. Specifically, PixelSR consists of two core designs: Pixel-space Conditional Diffusion and Orthogonal Flow Decoupling. Complementarily, the latter decomposes the asymmetric flow target into a low-rank semantic component and a noise-free orthogonal texture component. By routing these components to specialized branches, it achieves decoupled optimization with reduced mutual interference. Benefiting from these designs, our PixelSR restores perceptually realistic details while faithfully preserving structural content. Extensive experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that PixelSR not only achieves competitive performance, but also effectively mitigates the fidelity--realism seesaw effect.

Title: Testing TLS 1.3 PQC on constrained devices and microcontrollers

  • Authors: Olivier Gillot, William J. Buchanan, Callum Turino
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27766
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27766
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27766]] Testing TLS 1.3 PQC on constrained devices and microcontrollers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27766)
  • Keywords: secure
  • Abstract: The UK, EU and US governments have set timelines for post-quantum cryptography migration of critical systems, with deadlines ranging from 2028 to 2035. This paper focuses on IoT-class devices and microcontrollers and the viability of using PQC to connect these devices in secure TLS 1.3 connections. Using wolfSSL, we benchmark a 36-combination TLS 1.3 algorithm matrix, pairing ML-KEM-512/768/1024 and X25519/ECC P-256 key exchange with ML-DSA-44/65/87, Ed25519, ECDSA, and RSA-PSS authentication, across four devices (Raspberry Pi Pico W & Pico 2 W, ESP32, and a Raspberry Pi Zero), measuring both handshake latency and energy consumption. We found that in capable devices, all choices in the TLS matrix gave similar results, but that in IoT-class devices, PQC performance is highly implementation- and architecture-dependent: the common assumption that elliptic-curve key exchange must be cheaper than post-quantum lattice-based key exchange does not hold in this wolfSSL-based implementation, where the best full-PQC combination (ML-KEM-512 + ML-DSA-44) completes TLS handshakes 41% faster than the equivalent classical-modern pairing (X25519 + RSA-PSS) on the Pico W (up to 2.12x as energy-efficient, depending on the paired authentication algorithm), and 8.9x faster on the ESP32. Full-PQC, ML-KEM-512/ML-DSA-44, is not only possible but energy-competitive with or superior to Hybrid or Elliptic Curve alternatives on the most constrained devices, though ML-DSA's variable signing time means applications with strict latency-predictability budgets must accept this variance as the cost of full quantum-safe authentication. We further show that the IETF's draft-specified X25519+ML-KEM-768 hybrid incurs approximately the combined cost of its two components, rather than offering a performance compromise, making pure ML-KEM the more practical choice on hardware lacking Curve25519 assembly support.

  • Authors: Melika Shirian, Kianoosh Vadaei, Arshia Hemmat
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27773
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27773

  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: A global $\ell_1$ budget limits how much an adversary may change an image, not how that change is distributed. We ask how much of an $\ell_1$ attack's observed success remains when large coordinate changes are restricted. We add a per-coordinate cap to the budget, enforce the joint constraint by exact in-loop projection, and sweep the cap for APGD-$\ell_1$, SLIDE, and FMN-$\ell_1$ without altering their objectives. Across 18 standard-model settings, caps of at most $32/255$ retain a median $97\%$ of attack success relative to each method's native uncapped baseline, while median peak magnitude falls by $80\%$ and median effective support nearly triples. Direct in-loop constrained optimization preserves up to $88$ percentage points more attack success than post-hoc clipping, highlighting the importance of constraint enforcement. Suitable caps nevertheless vary across attacks and models, and adversarially trained classifiers exhibit more heterogeneous concentration sensitivity. These results establish perturbation concentration as a complementary, model- and attack-dependent axis of $\ell_1$ attack evaluation.

  • Abstract: 3D Human Pose Estimation (3D HPE) is used in indoor environments, such as smart homes, to analyze human movement and monitor human states.   • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) exhibit both knowledge of language, rendered by, for example, grammatical judgment ability, and abilities to perform tasks through language, such as math-word problem solving. Specifically, we examine the overlap and causal effects between the circuits extracted for linguistic and arithmetic tasks, using controlled materials and circuit discovery methods from the mechanistic interpretability field. Depth pruning offers a practical route to efficiency by directly reducing memory and latency without hardware-specific support. However, we observe that transformer layers exhibit markedly different task-dependent layer contributions, whereas existing depth-pruning methods ignore this heterogeneity and thus tend to optimize for a narrow domain or benchmark rather than preserve broad capability. This suggests that LLM depth pruning should be formulated as a multi-objective optimization problem rather than a single-score ranking problem. Across the Llama-3.1 and Qwen3 model families, SliMOO consistently achieves stronger multi-task trade-offs than rule-based, greedy, and scalarized baselines.   • Abstract: Multimodal retrieval-augmented generation over visually rich documents requires evidence at a finer granularity than entire pages. We introduce GARRET, a region-retrieval framework trained from (page, question, answer) triplets through differentiable generative feedback. A shared context-preserving retriever produces page and region multi-vector representations. During training, their retrieval scores define a soft routing distribution over candidate region crops. An auxiliary vision-language model attends to each crop separately, aggregates crop-specific contexts using the routing weights, and predicts the question followed by the answer under a next-token prediction objective. Gradients through the routing distribution assign query-conditioned credit to the indexed region representations, without requiring query-to-region annotations. At inference, the auxiliary model is removed. GARRET also reuses the contextualized region representations, together with page summary tokens, to construct a compact page representation. We introduce ViDoRe-V3-Region, a corpus-wide benchmark containing 200,682 fixed candidate regions across eight domains. GARRET achieves a macro nDCG@10 of 0.4126, outperforming the strongest visual baseline by 2.94 points. On page retrieval, it remains comparable to ColQwen2.5-v0.2 while indexing 47.8% fewer vectors per page.

Title: Manifold Prior Guided Deep Unfolding for Hyperspectral Image Reconstruction

  • Authors: Shuqi Liu, Yingkai Zhang, Lin Gu, Tao Zhang, Ying Fu
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27874
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27874

  • Keywords: extraction, interpretability
  • Abstract: Hyperspectral Image (HSI) reconstruction has made substantial progress with the deep unfolding framework by decomposing the problem into a projection module and a denoiser module. Nevertheless, existing methods still exhibit limitations of insufficient matching with HSI data. The issues lie in two aspects: 1) the data-consistency projection module applies a fixed gradient descent step while ignoring the spectral content adaptivity of HSI; 2) the denoiser module either suffers from high computational complexity or is limited to the local receptive field, failing to efficiently capture the global spatial-spectral dependencies. In the data-consistency projection, a prior-guided spectral weight mechanism is introduced to adaptively modulate the weight of spectral band. In the denoiser module, a manifold-guided Mamba is proposed to achieve the structure-aware extraction of spatial-spectral features through prior modulation. Quantitative and visual comparisons on synthetic and real-world datasets show that the proposed method significantly outperforms existing state-of-the-art approaches, while maintaining the structural interpretability of the unfolding framework. Title: Gaze2HOI: Gaze-Aware Intention Disambiguation for Egocentric Hand-Object Interaction Forecasting

  • Authors: Chuanyang Ma, Chenye Wang, Yixuan Wang, Huiling Zhou, Xingqun Qi, Muyi Sun, Man Zhang, Caifeng Shan, Zhenan Sun

  • Abstract: Anticipating how users will interact with their surroundings is essential for proactive systems that provide timely assistance in AR, VR, and embodied applications. Inspired by behavioral evidence that gaze can provide early cues for underlying intention, we propose Gaze2HOI, a gaze-aware framework that formulates hand-object interaction forecasting as a hierarchical problem comprising intention inference, contact grounding, and coupled hand-object motion generation. To evaluate Gaze2HOI, we further introduce EgoAIM, a phase-aware benchmark that organizes egocentric hand-object interactions into four phases defined by evolving hand-object relations and provides fine-grained intention annotations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Gaze2HOI achieves state-of-the-art performance in both intention inference and motion forecasting, even approaching oracle-level end-to-end performance in specific interaction phases.   • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Negation remains a persistent challenge for language models across NLP tasks and modalities.   • Authors: Mervat Abassy, Zhaoxin Feng, Tatsuki Kuribayashi, Kentaro Inui, Emmanuele Chersoni
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27928
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.27928
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.27928]] Why Do LLMs Accept Generics? Explaining Acceptability using Psycholinguistic and Model-Derived Factors(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27928)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Psycholinguistic studies explore why humans accept some generic statements but reject others by showing that these judgments depend on more than how common a property is. People consider several other factors such as how well it sets the kind apart from others or whether it is characteristic of the kind. Inspired by the linguistic and cognitive literature, we explore to what extent factors from these studies explain how large language models (LLMs) judge generics. Our results with twelve models on a psycholinguistic dataset and on naturally occurring generics show that models weigh how common a property is against how well it distinguishes the kind, and combine the two much as people do, though less clearly. Like people, they favor characteristic properties, but unlike the accounts of human judgment, they ignore striking ones. What matters most to LLMs is whether a statement is factually true, and much of the influence of human factors reaches their ratings through that judgment. In six 7-9B models, shifting the hidden states along each factor's direction moves the judgment as the factor predicts.

  • Abstract: Hyperspectral images provide a rich representation of the underlying spectrum for each pixel, allowing for a pixel-wise classification/segmentation into different classes. Spectral variability, mixed pixels, noise as well as high correlation between neighboring spectral bands make this task challenging. We present a feature-agnostic, unsupervised hyperspectral segmentation framework based on the piecewise-constant Mumford-Shah functional. Our framework turns a strictly proper scoring rule and a family of parametric probability distributions into an indicator function for the Mumford-Shah functional, and we naturally generalize some previous Mumford-Shah based models for image segmentation this way. Our focus will be on heavy-tailed elliptical distributions, the negative log-likelihood score and the score based on the beta-divergence to deal with the heavy outliers in hyperspectral imaging. Using the interplay between the calculus of variations and statistics, we also propose a robustness step to estimate the statistical parameters of these heavy-tailed elliptical distributions. In addition, an edge bias, which contains prior boundary information, is incorporated into both the data fidelity term and the perimeter term of the Mumford-Shah functional. Numerical experiments show that, to the best of our knowledge, our method achieves the highest overall accuracy among fully unsupervised methods on three of the four standard benchmarks (Indian Pines, Kennedy Space Center, Pavia University and Salinas) evaluated on the full scenes. It also improves upon our previous Mumford-Shah based model.

  • Abstract: Class unlearning for video segmentation aims to ensure that a removed category cannot be reconstructed from a deployed model's outputs. Distributed optimization addresses this challenge, with compression mechanisms helping to reduce communication costs. A classical tool for dimensionality reduction is the Johnson-Lindenstrauss (JL) transform, which is simple to implement and offers strong geometric guarantees. In particular, JL transforms approximately preserve inner products and pairwise distances between vectors with high probability. In this paper, we exploit this property in Byzantine-robust optimization, where worker messages are weighted according to their alignment with a trusted reference gradient. We further support our theoretical findings with experimental results.

Title: Beyond the Evidence Boundary: Behavioral Mirage Under Progressive Degradation in Medical VLMs

  • Authors: Edric Castel C. Hao, Rez Samantha Z. Floresca
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28000
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28000

  • Abstract: VLMs for medical image interpretation are typically evaluated on clean inputs, despite the progressive evidence loss that can occur in high stakes clinical settings. Title: P$^3$-VLM: A Point-based Alternative for Grounded 3D Vision-Language Models

  • Authors: Anna-Maria Halacheva, Jan-Nico Zaech, Sombit Dey, Luc Van Gool, Danda Pani Paudel

  • Abstract: Examining existing benchmarks for grounding in 3D VLMs reveals a pervasive "semantic leakage" in the evaluation and prompting protocols: a heavy reliance on bounding boxes as spatial prompts or representation primitives. The key benefit of point-based learning is a consistent improvement across settings: P$^3$-VLM achieves state-of-the-art performance under point-based grounding while also improving robustness, OOD generalization, and performance on ungrounded scene-centric 3D VQA benchmarks, reflecting its reduced reliance on bounding-box shortcuts and resulting stronger visual-spatial reasoning.

Title: Poisoning-Based Backdoors Are Not Cryptographic

  • Authors: Masahiro Kaneko
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28045
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28045
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28045]] Poisoning-Based Backdoors Are Not Cryptographic(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28045)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, large language model
  • Abstract: To defend against backdoor attacks on neural networks, the defender must identify the verifier function that determines whether an input is a trigger, rather than rejecting individual triggers in isolation, since an attacker can generate new triggers satisfying the same verifier faster than they can be blocked. If a backdoor verifier could be installed with the asymmetry of a cryptographic authentication scheme, in which only the attacker can produce trigger inputs while the defender cannot reverse-engineer the verifier from observations, learning-based defense would be impossible in principle. We show that no such asymmetry can arise from poisoning-based installation, in which the only verifier-dependent input to training is a poisoned dataset. The argument is a reduction. A training procedure that installs a verifier from poisoned data, so that the backdoor fires on unseen triggers while benign behavior is preserved, is itself a learner for that verifier. Any verifier that is computationally hard to learn is therefore impossible to install through poisoning, and any verifier that poisoning can install is reconstructible by a white-box defender holding comparably many labeled trigger observations. A complementary argument shows that a secret key supplied to the model at inference is observable to the same defender. The result takes representative trigger observations as given and concerns when the backdoor fires rather than what it does. Experiments on instruction-tuned large language models confirm the dichotomy, with no configuration yielding a backdoor that is both effective for the attacker and irrecoverable for the defender.

Using a controlled two-stage protocol in which both encoders are ultimately optimized for the same classification task, we enable a direct comparison of the resulting representations. We observe that candidate grids are not exchangeable views -- a coarse grid removes more structural information than a mildly resampled one -- yet standard consistency objectives assign them equal influence. We therefore propose LatticeRank, a training-only framework that aligns candidate posteriors in physical space and allocates consistency supervision by voxel-spacing order: among endpoint-matched candidates, the auxiliary loss concentrates on the coarsest requested grid, while inference retains a single network forward with no candidate branch or ensemble.   • Authors: Yousra Fettach, Tijl De Bie
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Persuasive writing exploits cognitive biases, recruiting a reader's mental shortcuts to shape belief, often without the reader noticing. We call the linguistic features exploiting such mental shortcuts cognitive bias triggers. As large language models (LLMs) increasingly mediate how people encounter news, summarizing it, ranking it, and answering questions over it, a natural question is whether they can recognize the features of a text that bias a human reader. We introduce BAIT (Bias Annotation In Text), a resource for detecting cognitive bias triggers in news. BAIT pairs a schema of nine triggers, each specified in an annotator codebook, with 50 English news articles annotated by trained annotators at the article and span levels and adjudicated into a gold standard. On this data we define two tasks, detecting which triggers a text contains and locating the spans that carry them. We then benchmark six frontier LLMs zero-shot and with three or ten in-context examples. The models approach human agreement on which triggers an article contains, but rarely recover its full set of triggers, and they reach less than half of human agreement in locating the passages that carry them. Moreover, they over-predict triggers that come with easy surface cues, and most of the passages they mark for these triggers are not triggers, yet carry the trigger's surface cue far more often than random text. These findings suggest that the evaluated LLMs respond to surface cues rather than to the mechanisms that make a passage manipulative. We release the schema, the codebook, standoff annotations, and the evaluation pipeline.

  • Keywords: protect, attack
  • Abstract: AI agents keep long-term memory, much of which comes from web pages and tool results. We propose ClawMMU, a protection layer that labels every piece of memory with its owner, scope, origins, and authority. Its central rule is that information may rise in authority, but authority never lifts itself. Title: OV-Foam: How 3D Representations Shape Semantic Feature Lifting

  • Authors: Yousef Rajeh (1), Alejandra Esparza Pelcastre (2), Ivan Viola (1), Dominik Engel (1) ((1) King Abdullah University of Science and Technology, (2) Universidad Panamericana)
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.GR

  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28131]] OV-Foam: How 3D Representations Shape Semantic Feature Lifting(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28131)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Existing feature lifting approaches for open-vocabulary segmentation rely on simple backprojection to assign features to primitives. In the case of 3D Gaussian splatting reconstruction, the primitives overlap heavily, leading to suboptimal reconstructions, governed by many primitives per ray. Backprojection, however, reduces to a least-squares solvable problem, exactly when no two primitives share a ray. Motivated by this insight, we propose OV-Foam, which uses PowerFoam as the underlying representation. This representation uses a power-diagram to partition the scene, resulting in zero overlap between primitives and a substantial reduction in primitives per ray, which ultimately simplifies the lifting problem and allows for closer-to-optimal feature assignment. We show that the space partitioning primitives of OV-Foam reach near-optimal assignment, and our experiments demonstrate improved open-vocabulary segmentation over Gaussian baselines.

  • Abstract: Flexible docking is a highly practical task, aiming to predict the joint configuration of a protein-ligand complex from a protein apo structure and a ligand SMILES string. Diffusion and flow-based generative models have made this task tractable by modeling the posterior distribution over the ligand pose and the protein conformation conditioned on the apo input. The central challenge, however, remains the long-tailed apo-to-holo structure shift, where a small fraction of protein undergo large conformational changes. In this work, motivated by the practical observation that downweighting large shift examples improves generalization, and by the mixed nature of the long tail consisting of genuinely difficult examples and noisy ones, we propose to use curriculum learning for this task, gradually incorporating large conformational change examples during training. With a linear pacing function, we show that an existing method can be strongly enhanced. On the PDBBind ESMFold apo benchmark, with relaxation and confidence-based pose selection, the resulting model GCLDock achieves a 46.52% top-1 success rate (ligand RMSD less than 2A) and 40.26% when additionally requiring PoseBusters validity, outperforming existing baselines.

Title: Temporal Evidence-Parameter Co-Alignment for LLM Reasoning over Temporal Knowledge Graphs

  • Authors: Jiapu Wang
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28176
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28176
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28176]] Temporal Evidence-Parameter Co-Alignment for LLM Reasoning over Temporal Knowledge Graphs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28176)
  • Keywords: interpretability, large language model
  • Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) often struggle with temporally grounded reasoning because their static parametric memory is misaligned with the dynamic evolution of real-world knowledge. Although RAG and Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) provide practical ways to enhance LLMs, they remain insufficient for temporal reasoning. Existing retrieval methods typically prioritize semantic relevance while overlooking temporal validity, event order, and evolutionary dependencies, resulting in evidence that is relevant in isolation but temporally fragmented. Meanwhile, conventional PEFT methods usually employ fixed and input-agnostic low-rank updates, making it difficult to capture time-dependent entity semantics and relation drift. To address these limitations, we propose Temporal Evidence-Parameter Co-Alignment (TEPCA), a framework for LLM reasoning over TKGs. TEPCA aligns LLM reasoning with temporal knowledge at both the evidence and parameter levels. At the evidence level, it introduces time-constrained Dynamic Relevance-Guided Path (DRGP) pruning to preserve globally coherent temporal paths, together with Mutual-Information (MI)-based pruning to retain query-informative local facts under temporal constraints. At the parameter level, it integrates Dynamic-Gated LoRA (DG-LoRA), a timestamp-conditioned low-rank adaptation mechanism that dynamically modulates the effective adaptation subspace according to temporal context. This design enables LLMs to better capture fine-grained entity evolution and time-dependent relation semantics while preserving parameter efficiency. Experimental results on two TKG benchmarks demonstrate that TEPCA achieves state-of-the-art performance. Additional evaluations of DG-LoRA on eight commonsense QA datasets further verify its general input-adaptive capacity, scalability across LLM backbones, and interpretability.

Title: Unified RAW Image Exposure Correction and Restoration via Linear Illumination and Frequency-Adaptive Enhancement

  • Authors: Yingkai Zhang, Yunhao Zou, Ying Fu
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28191
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28191

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Incorrect exposure degrades both image brightness and recoverable scene detail. Existing sRGB-based correction methods are limited by the non-linear image signal processing pipeline, whereas RAW-based methods are usually developed for a single degradation type, such as low-light denoising or high-light recovery. In this paper, we focus on joint exposure correction and restoration directly in the RAW domain and propose a unified RAW Image Exposure Correction and Restoration (RIECR) framework for both under- and over-exposed images. RIECR decomposes the problem into two coupled sub-problems that follow the RAW imaging model: linear illumination correction and residual degradation restoration. Second, a dual-branch Frequency-based Enhancement Network (FEN) restores the remaining degradation with a Frequency Dynamic Convolution (FDC) module. FDC decomposes convolutional responses into low- and high-frequency components and adaptively suppresses amplified noise or recovers clipped textures according to the exposure context. We further build a real paired RAW benchmark containing 223 scenes and 1,784 pairs covering complex multi-exposure errors. Experiments against sixteen state-of-the-art methods show that RIECR achieves state-of-the-art performance on real RAW exposure correction.

Title: Reframing Object-Centric Reinforcement Learning: From Absolute to Relative Representations

  • Abstract: Object-centric reinforcement learning agents typically receive each object's position in a fixed global frame, even though their decisions usually depend on where objects are relative to one another. Title: Can Language Models Get "Aphasia" From Neuronal Ablation?

  • Authors: Pratik Worah, Randall Bateman
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28214
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28214
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28214]] Can Language Models Get "Aphasia" From Neuronal Ablation?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28214)
  • Keywords: transformer, generative
  • Abstract: Progressive neuronal loss is the hallmark of neurodegenerative disease, yet how loss of neurons shapes the pattern of cognitive decline remains poorly understood. Here we systematically ablate neurons (computational units) in language models spanning recurrent long short-term memory (LSTM) and Transformer architectures and apply limited fine-tuning as a simple proxy for compensatory plasticity. With whole-unit ablation, LSTMs degrade gradually with syntactic and semantic faculties declining in parallel across mild-to-moderate damage. By contrast, Transformers exhibit late threshold-like collapse under feedforward multi-layer perceptron (MLP) ablation and smoother sigmoid-like decline under attention-head ablation. Qualitatively, MLP-ablated models continue to make within-category semantic substitutions (semantic paraphasias) alongside rising non-responses, paralleling Wernicke-type aphasia, while attention-head ablation produces a trend toward loss of semantic specificity similar to the semantic variant of primary progressive aphasia (svPPA). These findings indicate that feedforward and attentional pathways have functionally distinct failure modes, suggesting that architecture-dependent failure profiles may offer new computational perspectives on aphasic syndromes and neurodegenerative diseases as an in silico model. Notably, early accuracy retention under Transformer attention-head ablation qualitatively parallels pre-symptomatic reserve before the 40--60% neuronal loss reported by Gomez-Isla et al. (1996) when clinical symptoms emerge in Alzheimer's disease, whereas the steadier LSTM decline parallels the symptomatic progression described by DeKosky and Scheff (1990) and Terry et al. (1991).

Title: TPO: Tri-level Distributionally Robust Learning for OOD Direct Preference Optimization

  • Authors: Chengtao Jian, Kai Yang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28229
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28229
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28229]] TPO: Tri-level Distributionally Robust Learning for OOD Direct Preference Optimization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28229)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) minimizes empirical risk over observed preference comparisons, implicitly assuming that the training preference distribution matches the deployment preference distribution. In practice, this i.i.d. assumption often fails, as preference distribution shift exhibits a natural hierarchical structure across three levels: group-level shift, prompt-level shift, and preference-level shift. Such multi-level distributional mismatch poses a critical challenge for safe LLM deployment, yet existing robust DPO methods typically either model uncertainty over the joint preference distribution or focus on individual sources of shift, leaving the hierarchical structure of OOD preference shifts underexplored. We propose TPO, a tri-level distributionally robust framework for OOD preference alignment, which jointly models all three levels of distributional uncertainty through a hierarchically structured rectangular ambiguity set. By instantiating it with Wasserstein distance, we derive a tractable and interpretable objective that combines a closed-form preference-level margin penalty, prompt-level adversarial embedding perturbation, and worst-case group reweighting, each governed by an independent radius. We establish finite-sample convergence guarantees and empirically validate the effectiveness of TPO over existing robust DPO methods.

  • Abstract: Adapting Vision Foundation Models (VFMs) to medical imaging via LoRA has become standard practice, yet model selection is guided almost entirely by downstream task metrics, treating backbones with similar performance as interchangeable. Title: Disclosure Begets Disclosure: A Self-Reinforcing Privacy Loop in LLM Agents with Persistent Memory

  • Authors: Masahiro Kaneko, Hiromi Arai, Timothy Baldwin
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28315
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28315
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28315]] Disclosure Begets Disclosure: A Self-Reinforcing Privacy Loop in LLM Agents with Persistent Memory(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28315)
  • Keywords: privacy, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use persistent memory to personalize responses, and personal attributes in this memory pose a privacy risk if exposed. We ask how consulting memory during dialogue changes what users go on to say, and thus what the memory comes to reveal. In multi-session simulations, a simulated user with 24 personal attributes talks with a support agent that does or does not consult memory, and in both settings attributes are inferred from the full accumulated record. Across four support models, memory raises response quality but also empirical leakage, the share of attribute values correctly inferred from the record, by a margin that widens over sessions. The cause is a loop in ordinary, non-adversarial assistance. Memory-conditioned responses carry earlier values forward, often as presuppositions, and users affirm them or leave them uncorrected, so that cues and the agent's own guesses become recorded values. The loop reinforces itself, and removing the agent's mentions of an attribute lowers its later disclosure about twice as much as single-turn effects predict. Users cannot leave it on their own, since correcting a value discloses the attribute, declining to discuss it shifts later predictions toward sensitive values, and incorrect values left in place become entrenched. The loop is best weakened at the agent's response. Instructing the agent not to state or presuppose attributes brings leakage to about the no-memory level while keeping most of the quality gains, whereas filtering or anonymizing what users say or what is stored gives up much of them.

  • Abstract: Autonomous video editing from open-world resources remains a challenging problem in multimedia content creation. To address these limitations, we present Auto-Director, a tool-augmented multi-agent framework that mirrors professional editorial workflows: a Planning Agent decomposes abstract topics into fact-grounded scripts via retrieval-augmented planning; a Material Agent indexes open-world footage through shot segmentation and visual tagging; an Editor Agent assembles semantically aligned clips with MLLM-driven spatial adaptation; and a Critic Agent governs an iterative Reflect-and-Revise loop that checks scene-level alignment and pacing. To rigorously evaluate this new paradigm, we introduce VEdit-Bench, a benchmark of 100 expert-curated topics with human-annotated Golden Scripts and a five-dimensional metric suite covering Narrative Logic, Factual Accuracy, Semantic Alignment, Compositional Quality, and Stylistic Cohesion. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Auto-Director significantly outperforms baseline methods in both automated metrics and human evaluation, effectively bridging the gap between amateurs and professional-grade video production.

Title: Characterizing Cross-Relay Generalization Failure in Machine Learning-Based Power System Protection

  • Authors: Nithin Pradeep Nadayil Kizhakkethil, Julian Oelhaf, Christian Bergler, Andreas Maier, Siming Bayer
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28348
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28348
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28348]] Characterizing Cross-Relay Generalization Failure in Machine Learning-Based Power System Protection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28348)
  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: Machine learning-based fault detectors are often evaluated at the relay locations used for training, although local voltage and current measurements vary across power networks. This work investigates cross-relay generalization for binary detection of faults anywhere in the network using local measurements, rather than line-selective tripping. Using 50 ms windows from a simulated 110 kV transmission network, we evaluate a multilayer perceptron across all 64 source target combinations of eight relays and five random seeds. A shared simulation-level split and source-only threshold selection provide a controlled held-out evaluation without target-specific calibration. Despite nearly unchanged mean F1-scores (0.730 within-relay versus 0.734 cross-relay), the mean false-positive rate (FPR) increases from 0.088 to 0.341, while the mean false-negative rate decreases from 0.399 to 0.318. Linear probing indicates that learned representations retain coarse bus-group information, while exploratory class-conditional latent distribution analysis shows no consistent monotonic association with transfer errors. These findings demonstrate that aggregate metrics can conceal location-structured changes in false-alarm behavior, highlighting the importance of evaluating error types separately when assessing cross-relay generalization.

Title: CountVideoBench: A Benchmark to Evaluate Prediction Biases on the Object Counting task by Video-Language Models

  • Authors: Marc Chapus, Carlos Crispim-Junior, Véronique Eglin, Atilla Baskurt
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28356
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28356
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28356]] CountVideoBench: A Benchmark to Evaluate Prediction Biases on the Object Counting task by Video-Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28356)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Object counting in videos remains a poorly characterized capability of multimodal large language models. Counting questions are included in many existing VideoQA benchmarks, but the ground-truth count distributions in these benchmarks are heavily skewed toward small values. Across the nine benchmarks we survey, the median ground-truth count is at most five, and the three most frequent values together account for between 45% and 93% of the videos. The resulting mean accuracy is then dominated by the most frequent counts; a value-dependent bias at a rare count contributes negligibly to the average and remains invisible. We introduce CountVideoBench, a benchmark of 2,600 synthetic videos covering 26 cardinalities in the interval [5, 50] (100 videos each), grouped into six clusters of consecutive counts that span the range while enabling near-neighbor comparisons. Each video contains a single class of static objects under a controlled camera motion, removing object motion and reducing recognition as confounds. We evaluate sixteen video-language models from nine architecture families in a zero-shot setting. No model exceeds 27.6% Exact Match. The dependence of accuracy on cardinality varies across models, ranging from monotonic decline to a non-monotonic peak-and-collapse pattern, which rules out increasing difficulty with cardinality as the dominant explanation of counting failure. Every model further concentrates its predictions on its own set of preferred integers, with accuracy peaks coinciding with these values and collapsing at adjacent counts. These findings highlight the need for balanced, diagnostic counting benchmarks to reliably assess this fundamental visual capability.

Title: Context-Aware Prompt Injection Detection via Small Language Models in RAG Pipelines: nokast-secureRAG

  • Authors: Abhishek Rai
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28364
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28364
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28364]] Context-Aware Prompt Injection Detection via Small Language Models in RAG Pipelines: nokast-secureRAG(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28364)
  • Keywords: secure, attack
  • Abstract: Local Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems are vulnerable to indirect prompt injection and retrieval poisoning, where malicious instructions in retrieved content are mistaken for trusted input. We present nokast-secureRAG, a roughly 0.5B-parameter small language model that acts as a semantic firewall between retrieval and generation. It jointly evaluates the user query and retrieved context, classifying the pair as safe, suspicious, or malicious. The model is trained using a teacher-judge-student pipeline: a 35B teacher generates 5,233 examples across four attack families, and an independent 120B judge re-labels them, achieving 95.0% agreement before student fine-tuning. On a held-out in-distribution test set, the model achieves 0.994 detection recall at a 0.026 false-positive rate and a 0.006 attack-success-rate proxy, outperforming a regex baseline, a public prompt-injection classifier, Llama Guard 3 1B, and its untuned version. Prompt-Guard-86M reaches 0.900 recall but has a 0.690 false-positive rate. On a judgment-flip test, where the same sentence is benign or malicious depending on context, the model correctly classifies both cases 75% of the time, outperforming the compared baselines. Ablations indicate that retrieved context carries the main attack signal, while query-context consistency reduces false positives. Single-request latency is 293-404 ms, above the target of 100 ms, while amortized throughput latency is 37 ms under batching. On the external PoisonedRAG benchmark, recall falls to near zero, indicating that fluent fact-poisoning attacks lie outside the detector's instruction-consistency threat model. We report this limitation alongside the in-distribution results. The model, data-generation pipeline, and evaluation harness are released open source.

Title: Relative Representations Enable Portable White-Box Monitoring Across Language Models

  • Authors: Tim Bakker, Christos Louizos
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28366
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28366
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28366]] Relative Representations Enable Portable White-Box Monitoring Across Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28366)
  • Keywords: robust, transformer
  • Abstract: Independently trained autoregressive language models often acquire similar capabilities, but it remains unclear whether their internal representations organize behaviorally relevant information in compatible ways. We show that models do in fact organize information similarly by using specific behavioral probes that operate on compatible representations. This unlocks portable white-box monitoring: a probe developed and validated on one model can be reused when the model changes, without requiring labeled target data. To overcome incompatible native activation coordinates, we express each example through its angular distances to a shared set of anchors. We introduce a global anchored construction that summarizes information from all transformer layers and removes model-specific layer selection, so the same frozen probe can be straightforwardly applied to another model. We evaluate cross-model probe transfer across monitoring tasks on trivia datasets, safety monitoring, and mathematical reasoning. On average, transferred global anchored probes preserve at least 85% of same-model probe AUROC over chance. Anchored probes also support transfer to related datasets, remain robust across model scale and prompt changes, and retain performance better than standard probes after model updates. Transfer is nevertheless task- and target-dependent and can fail even when the monitored property remains strongly decodable in the target model. Overall, global anchoring reveals substantial task-relevant compatibility across independently trained models and provides a practical basis for portable white-box monitoring.

Title: Don't Skip the Baseline: A Controlled Comparison of Dynamic Expert Skipping in MoEs

  • Authors: Jakob Steimle, Thomas Elsken, Daniel Ehrhardt, Lukas Rinder, Daniel Cremers, Xi Wang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28369
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28369
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28369]] Don't Skip the Baseline: A Controlled Comparison of Dynamic Expert Skipping in MoEs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28369)
  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: A growing body of work proposes dynamic expert-skipping methods for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models that adjust the number of experts activated per token during inference, aiming to preserve model quality while further reducing the number of active parameters. These methods have added calibrated importance scores, inter-expert similarity metrics, or learned per-token allocators in order to identify experts to skip. However, different calibration regimes, different reductions in the effective number of experts, and evaluation suites make comparisons difficult. We classify four representative prior methods into a unified taxonomy and benchmark them against a natural, simple baseline: statically reducing the number of experts $k$ (i.e., same reduced $k$ across all layers and tokens). We provide a fair comparison of these methods at matched expert-compute budget, on two modern MoEs (Gemma4-26B-A4B and Qwen3-30B-A3B) in a text-only setting and a contemporary vision-language model (Qwen3-VL) in a multimodal setting. Surprisingly, we find that the simple baseline is hard to outperform consistently across models, modalities, benchmarks, and calibration settings. The methods we benchmark often underperform the baseline and achieved gains, when present, are minor. We further find that the effective $k$ achieved (or optimized for) in calibration can diverge from the effective $k$ in testing, resulting in over- or under-estimating the compute at inference time. Based on these findings, we recommend a calibration-free per-modality static $k$ reduction and validate this on a held-out model and modality. We further argue that a static control at matched measured compute is the baseline any dynamic skipping method must beat.

Title: Rewards for Budget-Aware Multi-Turn Agents That Adapt to Arbitrary Test-Time Constraints

  • Authors: Tom A. Lamb, Adam Golinski, Eleonora Gualdoni, Sinead Williamson, Michael Kirchhof

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28409

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language model agents are often given an inference budget and asked to maximise success within it. A multi-turn agent has to allocate this budget across many environment interactions. An external harness can enforce the budget, but it remains unclear whether the agent itself can learn when and how much to spend. We ask whether agents can learn this allocation across dynamically specified budgets and which rewards best teach this behaviour. A sigmoid penalty for overspending most often achieves the highest within-budget success at an individual budget, while a fixed negative reward for overspending generally yields the strongest natural compliance. Combining penalties for overspend frequency and magnitude gives the smallest average gap to the best method at each budget. Overall, we show that a single agent can learn to allocate resources across turns and adapt its behaviour to user-specified budgets using budget feedback without external budget-allocation controllers.

  • Abstract: Modern LLM agents persist state across sessions via two channels: long-lived memory files silently prepended to the system prompt, and scheduled tasks waking the agent without user input. A one-time indirect prompt injection committed to either channel can re-fire across sessions long after the attacker leaves, threats we term carryover attacks. Existing prompt-injection benchmarks evaluate attacks only in the session where malicious content is first observed, leaving the gate governing when and how often a planted payload later re-fires unmeasured. We present CarryoverBench: 12 personal-assistant scenarios and 101 attack tasks across three trigger channels: Instant, same-turn IPI; Autonomous, heartbeat-scheduled; and Latent, memory-resurfaced. Across five frontier models, planted payloads re-fire without measurable decay over five rounds; conditional on successful planting, the trigger stage often provides little additional protection. Porting the Latent channel to a second runtime, Hermes, attenuates but does not eliminate the effect, suggesting that carryover defense requires runtime-layer controls in addition to prompt-level interventions.

Title: CLOSER: Evidence-Aware Active Visual Verification for Embodied Object-Goal Navigation

  • Authors: Pengying Wu, Mingye Jiang, Yunze Hu, Zhejia Wen, Kangjie Zhou, Zongru Yang, Zhe Peng, Chang Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.RO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28476
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28476
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28476]] CLOSER: Evidence-Aware Active Visual Verification for Embodied Object-Goal Navigation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28476)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Object-goal navigation (ObjectNav) requires an embodied agent to find an instance of a specified category in an unseen environment and stop near it. The agent typically relies on an object detector but must judge online from a limited egocentric video stream, sometimes a single frame, whether a detected candidate is the target. Detectors often confidently mistake similar objects, such as a sofa for a bed, and stopping at a false positive fails the episode. Prevailing systems also approach candidates greedily, leaving them occluded or partially observed. We present CLOSER, an evidence-aware active verification framework that treats stopping as closed-loop evidence acquisition, coupling vision-language model (VLM) verification with active perception. CLOSER fine-tunes an open-source VLM to label each view of a candidate tracked across frames as target, non-target, or uncertain, selects complementary views with an embedding-based diversity gate, and fuses their per-view probabilities with a lightweight network. If the fused judgment remains uncertain, a diffusion policy drives the agent toward the candidate using only the tracked mask and depth until the decision becomes decisive. CLOSER runs locally and plugs into existing ObjectNav systems without retraining them. On Gibson and Habitat-Matterport 3D, it raises average success rate by 3.6 points and success weighted by path length by 2.0 points, showing that verifying evidence before committing improves embodied navigation.

Title: Asymmetric Looped Transformers: Fixed Global Representations for an Iterative Decoder

  • Authors: Cheng Liang, Tengfei Zhang, Wenjie DU, Jianyu Wu, Changlian Ma, Shuchen Pu, Ya Zhang, Yanfeng Wang, Zhouhan Lin
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28489
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28489
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28489]] Asymmetric Looped Transformers: Fixed Global Representations for an Iterative Decoder(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28489)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Looped Transformers increase effective depth by repeatedly applying shared layers, offering a way to scale computation without increasing parameter count. However, full-stack looping places context construction and iterative state refinement within the same recurrent computation, without explicitly distinguishing their roles. We introduce the Asymmetric Looped Transformer (AsymLoop), which separates single-pass context encoding from recurrent state refinement. A single-pass causal encoder produces layer-specific Global key-value (KV) caches that remain fixed across iterations, while a weight-tied recurrent decoder generates Local KV from its evolving state. Joint attention over both sources under a single softmax allows each update to integrate persistent contextual information with the current intermediate state. For training, we apply a heavy-tail-guided layerwise learning-rate method to the encoder and decoder. Experiments on FineWeb-Edu across four model scales, from 60M to 1B, show consistently lower validation perplexity and higher average downstream accuracy than Full Loop under matched effective depth and training token budgets. At 1B, AsymLoop improves average zero-shot accuracy across seven commonsense reasoning benchmarks from 49.58% to 50.85%. Further experiments extend these benefits to longer training budgets, an OLMo-style backbone, and sparse mixture-of-experts models. These results support asymmetric context encoding and state refinement as an effective design for recurrent language models.

Title: Present but Hard to Read: Probing Latent Reasoning with Minimal Pairs

  • Authors: Aur Shalev Merin
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28498
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28498
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28498]] Present but Hard to Read: Probing Latent Reasoning with Minimal Pairs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28498)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Probing studies of latent reasoning often read a model's state where the answer begins and take a failed probe as evidence that a value is absent. We test this with a digit copied from the question, in the looped model Ouro-1.4B and the standard transformer Qwen2.5-1.5B. Comparing prompts that differ only in that digit shows it is present at the last prompt token, in a code shared across questions (readout about 0.9). A probe that sees one prompt at a time, the usual setting, barely reads it: 0.36 in Ouro and 0.19 in Qwen even after 5,249 training questions. The digit's change is small rather than crowded: random noise lands on equally busy directions, while a random word in its place moves the state far more and is readable. Since every varied token leaves a trace, a successful probe is ambiguous too. We give a protocol, including a synthetic null for generic mixing, for testing computed values against copies and mixtures of the inputs. In pre-registered studies of two-step problems it finds a small signal for the unwritten intermediate a+b just before Ouro writes the answer's last digit, and its sign follows the operation, though mixing alone produces much of that sign-following part. Holding that value at another problem's from the first loop on does not move the written digit toward the other problem's, while the same patch of the answer does: the value is readable but not used.

Title: Binary Discrete Diffusion: Candidate-Set Diffusion for Logic and Planning Tasks

  • Authors: Rodrigo Casado Noguerales, Dimitri von Rütte, Bernhard Schölkopf, Thomas Hofmann, Aran Raoufi
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28543
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28543
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28543]] Binary Discrete Diffusion: Candidate-Set Diffusion for Logic and Planning Tasks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28543)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Discrete diffusion models iteratively denoise a noisy sequence of categorical data such as text, enabling any-order generation with self-correction and in-filling capabilities. This makes them promising candidates for reasoning, planning, and constrained-generation tasks like coding and math. Yet masked and uniform diffusion erase a token's identity in a single corruption event, so their noisy states cannot explicitly represent partially resolved uncertainty in which several alternatives remain plausible. We close this gap with Binary Discrete Diffusion, a family of continuous-time discrete-diffusion processes whose noisy state is a set of candidate tokens per position, represented as a binary membership vector. Binary diffusion changes candidate sets through additions and removals, allowing information to be lost and recovered gradually while the diffusion state itself represents partial uncertainty. On Sudoku-Extreme, Boxoban, and graph coloring, the symmetric formulation of binary diffusion consistently outperforms strong, well-tuned masked and uniform baselines under matched architectures and controlled training, sampling, and tuning budgets, with its largest gains on the hardest instances, while remaining competitive on language modeling (Text8, TinyStories, OpenWebText). These results support candidate sets as an effective intermediate representation for iterative reasoning and show that the corruption kernel can materially change the computation learned by the reverse model.

Title: Discount Yourself: Self-inclusive Freshness-weighted Gossip for Decentralised Learning under Client Outages

  • Authors: Qiyuan Wang, Ke Xiao, Christos Anagnostopoulos
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.DC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28545
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28545
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28545]] Discount Yourself: Self-inclusive Freshness-weighted Gossip for Decentralised Learning under Client Outages(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28545)
  • Keywords: federate
  • Abstract: Decentralised federated learning (DFL) trains personalised models on devices that average with their neighbours rather than with a central server. Real devices, however, are often offline for hours or days, and a client returning from an outage can recover only by aggregating the models its neighbourhood holds. We observe that the stalest of these models is usually the client's own, a case that existing staleness-aware rules, which discount only the models a client receives, do not address. We formalise aggregation after an outage as estimating the state of a fully available network and derive three principles. First, the receiver must be discounted like any neighbour. Every rule that weights it at least as much as each neighbour has an error that grows without bound with the outage, whereas discounting all candidates by relative staleness keeps the error bounded. Second, staleness must be measured as inherited lag rather than age, since local training resets a model's age but not the lag it absorbed from stale neighbours. We introduce the effective lag, which tracks this quantity at the cost of one scalar per message. Third, the discount must be steep, because absorbed lag persists in every later round. These principles define Self-inclusive Freshness-weighted Gossip (SFG), a family of drop-in aggregation rules with the communication and computation of decentralised federated averaging, whose guarantees hold for the stale receiver itself. On three image benchmarks and eight outage processes, including real smartphone availability traces, the lag-based members SFG-L and SFG-Cut consistently outperform decentralised federated averaging and recent age-of-information rules, and ablations show that each principle is necessary. The analysis also explains why tuned limited merging from gossip learning is effective.

Title: The Token-Energy Law of LLM Decode: Active Bytes and Realized Time Account for Joules per Token

  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.DC, cs.PF
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28576
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.28576
  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28576]] The Token-Energy Law of LLM Decode: Active Bytes and Realized Time Account for Joules per Token(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28576)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Energy-efficient deployment of large language models requires understanding how model architecture, hardware, and serving settings shape energy per generated token. We introduce the Token-Energy Law, a compact, interpretable model of GPU-board energy per output token for dense, full-history decoding. It relates token energy to two core dimensions: active bytes and realized decode time. Active bytes describe logical byte demand per output, separating dense weights shared within a decode iteration from private key--value (KV) histories read and updated for each request. Realized decode time is the elapsed decode duration divided by the number of output tokens produced. We combine the two byte coordinates and time in a common affine form, with coefficients calibrated for each model and hardware/runtime configuration. This formulation explains how batching amortizes shared-weight work, longer attended histories increase private KV demand, and throughput determines the time-associated cost per output. Our experiments cover three models under BF16 decoding on NVIDIA Hopper and Blackwell GPUs, including two-GPU tensor parallelism on Hopper. Using fixed coefficients and observed decode time, held-out condition-mean energy estimates differ from measurements by at most 3.73%.

Title: Cache Engineering for Agents

  • Authors: Tianyu Fu, Ruoxi Wang, Zihan Min, Zhongqi Ren, Zhihao Li, Tengxuan Liu, Shengxu Mu, Hanling Zhang, Yu Wang
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28577

  • Copy Paste: [[2610.28577]] Cache Engineering for Agents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28577)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents can handle increasingly complex tasks, navigating long trajectories of observations and actions. As these trajectories grow, key-value (KV) cache I/O and storage become the dominant cost. Trajectories can also exceed the LLM's maximum context length, requiring control over active cache growth. Prior work uses context engineering to reduce cache size by trimming or summarizing history. However, modifying the trajectory prefix invalidates subsequent cached states and triggers costly re-prefilling of retained context. Task accuracy can also suffer when too little context is retained to limit re-prefill costs. We propose cache engineering as a new scheme. It allows agents to directly control their active KV cache without modifying the trajectory or re-prefilling retained context. Specifically, agents can proactively use drop to exclude selected cache from future attention and repos to adjust retained keys' position encodings. We formalize these cache operations and identify history dependence as a serving challenge: identical token sequences can yield different cache states under different cache operation sequences, making prefix matching insufficient for correct cache reuse. Thus, we co-design the AKV serving engine. It provides a stateless interface compatible with existing serving APIs, while internally managing stateful cache reuse, eviction, and restoration. Experiments show that a tool-response rolling-drop cache policy improves average benchmark scores by 1.7 points and reduces average cost by 27.7%, compared with conventional threshold-triggered summarization. With AKV, it achieves up to 1.2x the output throughput and a 1.5x speedup in P95 request latency. Recursive self-improvement of cache policies further reduces cost by 7.1% over expert design. Our code is at this https URL.
